<insert>Spencer</insert>
# A 
# Complete History
<insert>K</insert>
->OF<-
## MAGIC, SORCERY,
->AND<-
## WITCHCRAFT;
->CONTAINING,<-

I. The most authentic and best-attested accounts <note>original: "Relations"</note> of *magicians, sorcerers, witches, apparitions, spectres, ghosts, demons,* and other preternatural appearances.
II. A collection of several very scarce and valuable trials of *witches*, particularly that famous one of the **Witches of Warboys** <note>The Witches of Warboys were a family—Alice Samuel and her husband and daughter—executed in 1593 for allegedly bewitching the children of a local nobleman in Huntingdonshire.</note>.
III. An account of the first rise of *magicians* and *witches*; showing the contracts they make with the *Devil*, and what methods they take to accomplish their infernal designs.
IV. A full refutation <note>original: "Confutation"</note> of all the arguments that have ever been produced against the belief of *apparitions, witches, etc.* with a judgment concerning *spirits*, by the late learned *Mr. **John Locke*** <note>John Locke (1632–1704) was a hugely influential Enlightenment philosopher; his inclusion here was likely intended to give the work intellectual credibility.</note>.

---
->VOLUME I.<-
---

LONDON: Printed for *E. Curll* <note>Edmund Curll was a notorious London bookseller and publisher known for his controversial and sometimes "scandalous" publications.</note> at the *Dial and Bible*, *J. Pemberton* at the *Buck and Sun*, both opposite <note>original: "against"</note> *St. Dunstan’s Church* in *Fleet Street*; and *W. Taylor* at the *Ship* in *Paternoster Row*. 1715. Price of the two volumes 5s <term>shillings</term>.

### THE
# PREFACE
## TO THE
# READER.

The following pages—which contain a history of magic, sorcery, witchcraft, apparitions, ghosts <note>original: spectres</note>, and so on—we think it necessary to first give the reader a brief account of the nature and usefulness of our project <note>original: design</note>. Since these pages may be read by both the learned and the unlearned—some of whom are inclined to question truths, no matter how sacred or well-documented—having presented the purpose and usefulness of this treatise, we shall then offer evidence to support the belief and credibility of histories that are so well-recorded.

First, to describe the nature of our project, we will briefly explain the approach we have taken in this history and give the reader a short account of what to expect in the following pages. First, then, we have provided a concise <note>original: compendious</note> account of the origins and development of the art of magic and witchcraft, as an—

->*The Preface to the Reader.*<-

introduction to the following *History*, so that the reader might have a general overview <note>original: Notion</note> of the diabolical arts practiced by such wretched persons. It describes the manner in which they make their abominable contracts with the Devil and bring wicked spirits under their command to put their evil designs into practice. From there, we proceed to give a historical account of the mischievous proceedings and actions of those instruments of the Devil—magicians and witches—and what untimely and disgraceful ends such wicked practices eventually brought them to.

And because several *tracts* <note>original: Tracts; pamphlets or short books</note> have been published on these subjects—many of which are too wordy <note>original: prolix</note> and filled with long and tedious stories that are less worthy of our attention because they are less authentic and not well-documented—they are also filled with tiresome arguments that are hardly necessary to prove truths that are so obvious. In this *work*, we have included only those accounts that appear to be of beyond doubt in their credibility and authority, and which may be entertaining and amusing <note>original: diverting</note> as well as useful.

<margin><note>handwritten mark in margin</note></margin>As for the usefulness of this *treatise*, the *histories* contained in it have been collected from the best *authors* who have written on these subjects. These accounts not only serve to remind us of the *delusions* of *Satan* and the bad consequences that follow those who serve such an evil *master* as the *Devil*, but also show that the *Devil* does not have equal power to carry out his evil purposes on everyone indiscriminately. Rather, he can only act upon those whom *God Almighty* chooses to permit. Therefore, these stories may remind us to arm ourselves against the *temptations* of the *Devil* and to plead for *God Almighty’s assistance*, so that the *Devil* may have no power over us. And just as *theology* <note>original: Divinity</note> teaches us how to serve *God* and withstand the temptations of such a bad *master*, so the reading—

-># The Preface to the Reader.<-

<sig>A 4</sig>

...of these histories may increase our horror of such a great enemy of our salvation. It shows, through examples, how he constantly tries to disturb our peace <note>original: Quiet</note> and ruin our souls. This may also incline us to serve God—who is our Savior and protector from this grand adversary <note>A common term for the Devil</note>—with greater courage and zeal.

Having thus briefly explained the purpose and usefulness of this work, we shall next—for the sake of those who are less likely to believe truths of this kind, no matter how well-documented—offer evidence to confirm the belief and credibility of histories that are so well-supported. Just as we use the authority of the most respected writers, both ancient and modern, in the following histories—taking notice of none but those of certain credibility and strong evidence—we shall also cite the opinions of the best and most reputable authors to strengthen the believability and probability of these accounts.

To show what reason suggests concerning the existence of spirits and their operations, we shall first offer what Athanasius Kircher <note>A famous 17th-century German Jesuit scholar and polymath</note> writes on this occasion in his *Introductory Letter* <note>original: Epist. Parænet. (Epistola Paraenetica)</note> prefixed to his book *The Pamphilian Obelisk* <note>original: Obeliscus Pamphilius; a study of Egyptian hieroglyphs</note>. He says:

> "We know that a threefold demonstration has always been used by the unanimous consent of philosophers in the pursuit of knowledge <note>original: Science</note>: mathematical, physical, and moral.
> 
> Mathematical demonstration investigates the effects and properties of quantity using principles known by the light of nature—principles of eternal truth and free from all deceit. Thus, it produces a certain and true knowledge, removing all doubt. 
> 
> Physical demonstration arrives at the secret knowledge of causes through experiments with physical things. It does indeed produce knowledge, but because experiments, for the most part...

<meta>This page continues the quote from Athanasius Kircher regarding the three types of demonstration (mathematical, physical, and moral). -># The Preface to the Reader.<-

> "[Physical demonstration] part is exposed to the false representations of the senses; it is not free from deception, nor does it reach the certainty of the former <note>referring to mathematical demonstration</note>. Moral demonstration, because it depends on the experience of human actions, produces knowledge <note>original: Science</note>, but only such knowledge as the nature of moral matters allows. This is called human faith, and for the most part, it relies on the authority of the person reporting the event. For the same reason, the authority of the Revealer [God] produces divine faith, which is more certain than all scientific knowledge. Human authority is a kind of imitation of this, and we must rely on it unless we wish to invalidate and wipe out the histories of all past events. I am not speaking here of the authority and histories of questionable credibility, but of those which have been validated by the clear testimony <note>original: Prescription</note> of many centuries."

Building upon this fundamental thesis, we shall add what a learned author says, namely: "Keeping this before us, it should be noted that Christian theologians <note>original: Divines</note> do not claim to have a mathematical or physical demonstration of the existence of spirits. Their existence cannot be proven from their essence, nor from the effects attributed to them. 
> 
> It cannot be proven from the first (their essence), because it is not inherent in the nature of spirits—nor in the nature of any other created beings—that they must exist. God created all beings so freely that he might have left them uncreated. Nor can it be proven from their effects, because we could imagine that the direct involvement <note>original: Concourse</note> of God alone, or other causes, might be enough to produce such effects. 
> 
> Instead, Christian theologians rely chiefly on divine revelation, which is superior to all science, and on the constant tradition of all Christian thinkers from the earliest ages of Christianity. And all they claim as natural philosophers <note>original: Physiologers</note> regarding spirits is that there is nothing in the concept that implies a contradiction."

<summary>This page concludes Athanasius Kircher's argument that historical accounts rely on "moral demonstration" and introduces a theological argument that the existence of spirits is proven through revelation and tradition rather than mathematical or physical science.</summary>
<keywords>moral demonstration, divine faith, human authority, spirits, theology, revelation, Athanasius Kircher, natural philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a block quotation arguing that the existence of spirits is a reasonable hypothesis supported by both divine revelation and the most learned ancient philosophers.</meta>

-># The Preface to the Reader.<-

> "...is inconsistent with reason. Since there have been, and still are, many phenomena <note>original: Phænomena; observable events or marvels</note> in the world which philosophers have felt necessary to explain, the doctrine of the existence of spirits has been introduced as a hypothesis. This idea has been supported by as great men among the Gentiles <note>non-Christian or pagan philosophers of antiquity</note> as the world has ever known. Although other philosophers have proposed different hypotheses to explain these phenomena, I believe it would be strangely reckless for any person who accepts the Law of Moses or Christ to set aside a hypothesis supported by divine revelation—especially one that was also introduced by the most learned Gentiles in a way consistent with that revelation. To instead cling to any other hypothesis created only by human wit—which neither accounts for phenomena as well as the first, nor justifies shamefully denying facts that must be explained—is a sign of boundless overconfidence. I have seen such confidence even among Christians, whereas Vanini <note>Lucilio Vanini (1585–1619), an Italian philosopher executed for his radical views</note>, who died a martyr for atheism, and Pomponatius <note>Pietro Pomponazzi (1462–1525), a Renaissance philosopher known for skepticism regarding the soul</note>, who has been considered by some to share that opinion, along with many others, freely admit to facts they found undeniably demonstrated by experience and testimony, even though they did not choose to explain them through the agency of spirits."

To show the reasonableness of the hypothesis of spirits, Plutarch introduces Cleombrotus <note>original: Gleombrotus</note>, who expresses himself this way: "Just as those say very well who hold that Plato—by describing that element from which qualities spring and are produced <note>original: engendred</note>, which is sometimes called the 'first matter' and sometimes 'nature'—has freed philosophers from many difficulties; so it seems to me that those who have introduced the nature of spirits <note>original: Dæmons; referring to the Greek concept of beings between gods and mortals</note> between that of the gods and men, have solved many more and even greater doubts and difficulties..."

-># The Preface to the Reader.<-

...having found the bond <note>original: Tye</note> which joins and holds together, as it were, our society and communication with them. Whether this opinion came from the ancient Magi and Zoroaster <note>original: Zoroastres; the ancient Persian prophet</note>, or from Thrace <note>original: Thracia</note> and Orpheus, or from Egypt, is doubtful; we rather conjecture it comes from Phrygia, considering the sacrifices that are made in those countries.

Father Le Brun <note>Pierre Le Brun (1661–1729), a French Oratorian priest who wrote extensively on the divining rod and superstition</note>, having set forth many strange <note>original: odd</note> discourses on hidden things revealed by the conjuring wand <note>original: Conjuring Wand; refers to the divining or dowsing rod used to find water, minerals, or even criminals</note>, examines the causes of its turning during these discoveries. Having soundly refuted all natural causes that others have attempted to assign to it, he concludes it is moved by the agency of evil spirits, whose existence he proves as follows:

> If there are effects which cannot be produced by physical bodies, there must necessarily be other beings in the world besides bodies. And if among these prodigious <note>original: prodigious; marvelous or unnatural</note> effects, there are some that do not lead men to God but instead make them fall into error and illusion, it is a further undeniable <note>original: invincible</note> argument that we must acknowledge other beings besides physical bodies and the absolutely perfect Being <note>God</note>. Thus, those extraordinary effects—which can neither be doubted nor attributed to God or to physical bodies—are incontestable proof that we must admit the existence of created spirits capable of distracting <note>original: amusing</note> and seducing men with deceits. Therefore, even if the Scriptures had not clearly taught us the existence of spirits separated from bodies, I dare say that extraordinary effects, such as the discovery of many hidden things by the turning of the conjuring wand, would give strong proof that there are wicked spirits. But their existence is clear enough in the Scriptures; and certainly, it is the best established of any article of our faith, the least contested, and the most universally spread throughout the world.

> "Maimonides, in his *Guide for the Perplexed* <note>original: *More Nevochim*; a 12th-century work of Jewish philosophy</note>, proves with great learning and judgment that before the time of Moses, the Sabaeans, Egyptians, and Chaldeans knew of good and evil spirits; all the ancient poets and philosophers acknowledged <note>original: owned</note> this truth. We find in the history of the conversion of various peoples that this belief has always been established among remote nations. Nor can it be said that this is merely the ignorance <note>original: stupidness</note> of certain nations, for the most refined <note>original: polite</note> people do not differ in this regard from those they call 'barbarians.' We may see in the works of Porphyry <note>original: Porphyrius</note>, Iamblichus <note>original: Iamblicus</note>, and Clement of Alexandria <note>original: Clemens Alexandrinus</note> how similar the doctrine of the Greeks was to that of the Egyptians concerning the existence of spirits.
> 
> The newly converted Christians of the early Church <note>original: Primitive Times</note>—who, having been freed from the follies of paganism, were watchful of the Gentiles to observe their practices—acknowledged that sometimes wonders <note>original: prodigies</note> were performed by demons <note>original: dæmons</note>. Minucius Felix <note>original: Minutius Felix</note>, who lived in the second century, has very effectively set forth the views <note>original: Sense</note> of the Christians of those times concerning the nature and operations of those demons whom the Gentiles worshiped. 
> 
> Tertullian, Origen, and almost all the writers of the first three centuries have delivered the same message with all the assurance that truth can provide. What these great men have said is a very good answer to the objection sometimes raised: that Christ destroyed the kingdom of Satan and that 'the prince of this world is now judged' (John 16:11 <note>original text cites Job 16:11, but the quote is from the Gospel of John</note>). St. Peter, St. Paul, and St. John—men well-instructed in the words of Christ and the meaning that ought to be given to them—tell us that the Devil, like a roaring lion, goes about to deceive us. They teach us that we ought to have recourse to prayer to keep us firm in faith and to preserve us from his tricks <note>original: artifices</note> and the snares...

"...the snares he lays for us. The Devil, therefore, has not left the world so as to no longer act, but has been driven from many places where he once ruled. It is a truth of our faith that God has left some power to devils, and he permits them on many occasions to put it into execution. The frequent cases of demonic possession in the early ages of the Church, along with the most reliable histories recorded since the time of Christ, are authentic testimonies of this. Additionally, a thousand superstitious practices that produce extraordinary effects provide us with incontestable proof of the operation of *demons* <note>original: Dæmons</note>. And is there any ecclesiastical <note>church-related</note> writer who has not either proved or assumed this truth?

The learned *Gerson* <note>Jean Gerson (1363–1429), a prominent French scholar and Chancellor of the University of Paris</note> tells us what we ought to believe in this case, and why it is that this truth makes so little impression on the minds of many people. He says that it is certainly an act of impiety and an error directly contrary to the Scriptures to deny that *demons* are the authors of many surprising events. Those who view everything said about this as a mere fable, and who mock theologians <note>original: Divines</note> for ascribing these effects to *demons*, deserve a severe correction. 

Sometimes even the learned fall into this error because they allow their faith to be weakened and their natural reason <note>original: natural Light</note> to be darkened. Their souls, being entirely preoccupied with things perceived by the senses, refer everything to physical bodies and cannot elevate themselves to consider spirits that are detached from matter. This is what *Plato* said: nothing hinders the discovery of truth as much as referring everything to what the senses present to us. *Cicero*, *St. Augustine* <note>original: St. Austin</note>, *Albertus Magnus*, *William of Paris* <note>original: Gulielmus Parisiensis; a 13th-century philosopher and bishop</note>, and above all, experience, have taught us the same. We can see proof of this in the *Sadducees* and the *Epicureans*—who, by admitting nothing but what is physical <note>original: corporeal</note>, find themselves among those senseless people of whom *Solomon* speaks in *Eccle-*

"...[Ecclesi]astes <note>The text likely refers to the biblical book of Ecclesiastes here, continuing from the previous page</note> and the Book of *Wisdom* <note>A book of the biblical Apocrypha often cited in arguments regarding the soul and the afterlife</note>, who have pushed their folly so far that they cannot admit they have a soul, nor acknowledge effects that can only be produced by spirits. I wish there were no more people with that mindset; however, we will always find some who will tell us quite calmly <note>original: "in cold Blood"</note> that they cannot believe in wonders <note>original: "Prodigies"</note> or miracles because they have personally seen nothing extraordinary. Do not argue with such people. When a person chooses to be skeptical, they will remain so even in the midst of wonders and miracles. There are always people with the temperament of *Celsus* <note>A 2nd-century Greek philosopher and early opponent of Christianity</note> and *Lucian* <note>Lucian of Samosata, a 2nd-century satirist known for his skepticism toward the supernatural</note>, who insist that everything is a fable, an illusion, or a fraud <note>original: "Imposture"</note>. Many people measure everything by what they commonly see and consider everything false that exceeds the boundaries of nature. They believe facts as long as they appear natural; once you convince them that the facts cannot be natural, you will see them immediately conclude that they are frauds.

It is obvious that we can only conceive of two types of beings: *spirits and bodies*. Since we can only reason based on the ideas we have, we ought to attribute to spirits whatever cannot be attributed to physical bodies."

The author of the *Republic of Learning* <note>Referring to *Nouvelles de la République des Lettres*, an influential journal of the period</note>, having proposed a draft for writing a high-quality treatise on *witchcraft* (which he considers a much-needed work <note>original: *Desideratum*</note>), writes among other things that since this age is well-supplied with good systems <note>The author uses "systems" to refer to organized philosophical or scientific frameworks</note>, he wonders why no one has devised a system concerning the communication <note>original: "Commerce"</note> that may exist between *demons* and men.

To which Father *Le Brun* answers: "Doubtless, here the author is following the language of many people who, for lack of focus and insight, would have us reduce all religion to *systems*. Regardless of the respect I should have for many of these people, I

"...we must not be afraid to say that there are no scientific systems to be made for those truths which we ought to learn specifically by faith, because we must propose nothing here except what we receive from the divine word <note>original: "Oracle"</note>. We must create systems to explain the magnet <note>original: "Loadstone"</note>, the ebbing and flowing of the sea, and the motion of the planets. The causes of these effects are not clearly revealed to us, and many must be conceived by our own reasoning; to reach a conclusion, we need a great number of observations so that an exact logical induction <note>original: "Induction"; the process of reaching a general conclusion from specific facts</note> may lead us to a cause that explains all the observable events <note>original: "Phænomena"</note>.

It is not the same with the truths of religion; we do not arrive at them by fumbling around in the dark <note>original: "Groping"</note>. It is to be wished that men would not speak of them except by following a decisive and infallible authority. This is how we should speak of the power of demons <note>original: "Dæmons"</note> and the interactions <note>original: "Commerce"</note> they have with humans. It is a matter of faith that they possess power, that they attack people, and that they try to seduce them in several ways. We find this in the books of *Job* and *Tobit* <note>A book of the biblical Apocrypha</note>, and in a thousand other places in Scripture and tradition.

It is also certain that the power they possess does not depend on us. They have power even over the righteous <note>original: "the Just"</note>, since they may tempt them just as they tempted *Christ*. However, they do not usually have this power except over those who lack <note>original: "want"</note> faith or do not fear to participate in their evil works. To these latter people, these disordered intelligences <note>a term for fallen angels or demons</note> especially try to ensure that whatever they wish for succeeds exactly. They inspire such people to resort to certain practices through which those seducing spirits enter into dealings with humans. All of this is discovered without the need for a scientific system." So far, the arguments of Father *Le Brun*.

As for those philosophers who reject the existence of spirits primarily because they claim they can have no concept <note>original: "Notion"</note> of such a thing as a spiritual substance, I think the late Mr. *Locke*, in his detailed *Essay on Human Understanding* <note>John Locke’s influential 1689 work on the nature of knowledge and the mind</note>, has clearly demonstrated that humans have

<meta>This page continues the transition from Father Pierre Le Brun’s religious arguments to a philosophical defense of the soul. It quotes an unnamed author (John Locke) to argue that our understanding of spiritual "substance" is just as logical and valid as our understanding of physical matter.</meta>

[...people] have as clear a notion of a spiritual substance as they have of any physical <note>original: "corporeal"</note> substance, matter, or body. There is as much reason to believe in the existence of the one as there is for the other; therefore, if they do not admit the latter, it is merely stubbornness <note>original: "Humour"; here meaning a persistent whim or irrational inclination</note> in them to deny the former. He reasons this way:

> "If a man examines himself concerning his notion of pure substance in general, he will find he has no other ideas of it except by a supposition of 'he knows not what' support for the qualities that are capable of producing simple ideas in us. These qualities are commonly called accidents <term>accidents</term> <note>In traditional philosophy, "accidents" are properties like color, weight, or size that can change without changing the essential nature of the "substance" they belong to.</note>. Thus, if we talk or think of any particular physical substance, such as a horse or a stone, although the *idea* we have of either of them is only the combination or collection of those several simple ideas or sensory qualities which we are used to finding united in the thing called a horse or a stone, yet because we cannot imagine how they could exist alone, nor how one could exist within another, we suppose them to exist in and be supported by some common subject. We call this support 'substance,' though it is certain we have no clear or distinct idea of that thing we assume is a support.
> 
> The same happens concerning the operations of our mind—namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, thinking, reasoning, fearing, and so on <note>original: "&c."</note>. Since we conclude these cannot exist by themselves, and we do not understand how they can belong to a physical body, we are inclined to think these are the actions of some substance we call spirit. From this it is evident that we have no other notion of matter than as something in which many sensory qualities (which affect our senses) exist. Therefore, by supposing a substance in which thinking, knowing, doubting, and the power of movement exist, we have as clear a notion of the nature or substance of spirit,"

<meta>catchword: as</meta>

<summary>This page presents a philosophical argument, likely from John Locke, asserting that our concept of a spiritual "spirit" is just as valid as our concept of physical "matter," since both are based on the logical assumption of an underlying substance that supports observable qualities.</summary>

# The Preface to the Reader.

' as we have of the Body; the one being supposed
' to be (without knowing what it is) the *underlying foundation* <note>original: "Substratum"</note>
' to those simple ideas which we receive from the
' outside; and the other supposed (with an equal
' ignorance of what it is) to be the *underlying foundation*
' of those operations which we experience <note>original: "experiment"</note> in
' ourselves within. It is plain then, that the idea
' of physical <note>original: "corporeal"</note> substance in matter is as remote
' from our conceptions and understanding as
' that of spiritual substance; and therefore, from
' our not having any notion of the substance of
' Spirit, we can no more conclude its *non-existence*
' than we can for the same reason deny the ex-
' istence of the Body; it being as rational to affirm
' there is no Body because we cannot know its
' essence, as it is called, or have the idea of the
' substance of matter, as to say there is no
' Spirit because we know not its essence, or
' have no idea of a spiritual substance.'

Mr. *Locke* <note>original: "Lock"</note> also, comparing our idea of Spirit with our idea of the Body, thinks there may seem to be even less obscurity in the former than in the latter. Our idea of the Body he takes to be an extended solid substance, capable of communicating motion by impact <note>original: "Impulse"</note>; and our idea of the Soul is a substance that thinks, and has a power of initiating motion in the Body by will or thought. Now some perhaps will say they cannot comprehend a thinking thing, which perhaps is true; but he says, if they consider it well, they can no more comprehend an extended thing. And if they say they know not what it is that thinks in them, they mean they know not what the substance is of that thinking thing; no more, says he, do they know what the substance is of that solid thing. And if they say they know not how they think, he says neither do they know how they are extended, or how the solid parts of the Body are united to make extension, etc.

<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<summary>This page presents John Locke's argument that the existence of the soul (spiritual substance) is just as logically sound as the existence of the body (physical substance), as the inner essence of both remains unknown to human perception.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that mental functions cannot be explained by physical matter alone. It draws on the works of Jean Le Clerc and Ralph Cudworth to support the idea that because matter lacks the capacity for thought, the mind must originate from a higher, non-material source.</meta>

# The Preface to the Reader.

In agreement with what Mr. Locke <note>original: "Lock"</note> has presented here, Monsieur *Le Clerc*, in his *Coronis* <note>Jean Le Clerc’s *Coronis* (1710) was an appendix to his work on logic</note>, argues as follows: 

> “When we contemplate physical <note>original: "corporeal"</note> nature, we can see nothing in it but extension, divisibility, mobility, and various measurements of quantity or shapes. Since this is the case, it would be a reckless thing, contrary to the laws of sound reasoning, to claim other qualities for physical bodies. Consequently, we cannot deduce anything from mere matter that is not necessarily connected to those aforementioned properties. Therefore, those who have thought that the properties of sensory perception, understanding, willing, imagining, remembering, and other similar powers—which have no similarity to physical things—arise from the body, have greatly failed in the method of correct reasoning and philosophy. This error was made by *Epicurus* <note>An ancient Greek philosopher who proposed that the soul was made of physical atoms</note> and his followers, who claimed that our minds are composed of physical atoms. But from where should we say these powers arise? Truly, they do not owe their origin to matter, which is completely lacking in sensation and thought; nor did they spontaneously spring from nothing, it being a widely accepted and obviously true *maxim* that *nothing comes from nothing* <note>original: "Nothing sprung from nothing"; a translation of the Latin philosophical principle *ex nihilo nihil fit*</note>.”

Therefore, the learned Dr. *Cudworth* <note>Ralph Cudworth (1617–1688), a leading Cambridge Platonist philosopher</note>, in his *Intellectual System of the Universe* <note>original: "Intellectual System of the World"</note>, seeing nothing in matter but what we have mentioned and considering the previously stated maxim, moved from the consideration of physical bodies to the contemplation of a much more excellent nature. By this nature, he and other *natural philosophers* <note>original: "Physiologers"</note> understood that human minds and all other intelligent spirits were created. Therefore, the consideration of physical nature, joined with the knowledge of the mind's properties, leads people the direct

<meta>catchword: Way</meta>

<summary>This page argues that mental faculties like understanding and will cannot arise from physical matter, citing Jean Le Clerc's critique of Epicurean atomism and Ralph Cudworth's philosophical system. It concludes that the existence of the mind points toward a higher, non-material creative source.</summary>

# The Preface to the Reader.

<meta>catchword: Fourthly,</meta>

...leads us to the two most important principles <note>original: "Tenets of the greatest Moment"</note>: namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, the existence of a supreme God and the creation of the human soul by God, from which we also conclude its immortality. To this we may add that, although the authority of divine revelation is worthy of belief on its own, it is greatly strengthened in our minds when we see that revelation and reason both work together to confirm our faith.

The previously mentioned Mr. Le Clerc <note>Jean Le Clerc (1657–1736), a Swiss theologian and philosopher</note> has presented several points concerning spirits and how they operate, which we will list as the following main points to help guide our judgment when considering their nature.

<margin>‘</margin>**First:** Those who claim—or deny—that spirits can exist without any physical <note>original: "corporeal"</note> qualities go further than they should. We cannot conclude from the nature of spirits whether they exist entirely without physical properties or if they possess a "subtle body" <note>A common early modern concept of a spiritual body made of extremely fine, invisible matter</note>.

<margin>‘</margin>**Second:** Regarding the appearances <note>original: "Apparitions"</note> of spirits, he says we cannot prove by reason alone that it is impossible for spirits to be joined to a subtle body. Furthermore, it is unlikely that so many nations, located in such distant places and holding such different opinions, would all agree on a lie regarding everything they have said about the appearance of spirits. It is much more likely that the source of the lies invented about this matter was some true appearance, which—as usually happens—was then followed by a world of other fictional stories of the same kind.

<margin>‘</margin>**Third:** We are so far from being able to define the nature of an angel’s way of thinking <note>original: "intellection"</note> that we do not even fully understand the nature of our own thinking.

# The Preface to the Reader.

' Fourthly, regarding what the **Gentiles** <note>original: "Gentiles"; here referring to the ancient Greeks and Romans</note> say about **demons** <note>original: "Dæmons"; in this period, the term often referred to spirits in a general sense, not exclusively evil ones</note>, and what the **Hebrews** say about angels, there is nothing contrary to any certain knowledge we possess; therefore, what they say may be true, if the matter is considered on its own merits.'

' Fifthly, while it cannot be doubted that there may be many errors in accounts of the strange performances of witches based on a contract with demons, it would be rash to dismiss all of them as falsehoods—especially since the Scriptures relate similar things. Furthermore, the nature of demons is not so well known to us that we can definitively conclude that their nature is incompatible with many of the things reported about them. If anyone were to argue—because both good and evil angels are believed to be "thinking substances" <note>A philosophical term, often associated with René Descartes, referring to an entity whose whole essence is thought</note>—that they have no power over physical bodies because pure thought alone has no power over bodies, he would first need to show, and show clearly: *First*, that there is nothing to an angel besides thought; and *Secondly*, that there is no connection established by God between their wills and certain changes in physical bodies. For if either of these possibilities may be admitted without absurdity, they may also be thought capable of acting on bodies without absurdity.'

' Sixthly, some say that no true miracles, but only tricks <note>original: "Cheats"</note>, are performed by **demons**. However, to understand what this means, we must define the words being used: a portent, a miracle, or a prodigy are all the same thing in this context; they signify an effect that is: *First*, above human power; *Second*, outside the constant course of nature; and *Third*, done at a man’s pleasure, or at the exact moment he wishes. Now, who can prove through certain arguments that nothing can be done by evil demons that is above hu-

# The Preface to the Reader.

> ‘...human power, beyond the usual course of nature, at the moment the magician pleases, since the limits of angelic power are unknown? We can assert nothing here except from experience.
> 
> ‘ *Seventhly*, those who deny certain wonderful facts mostly contend they are *illusions* <note>original: "Praestigia"; a Latin term for deceptions, juggler's tricks, or magical illusions</note>; but besides the fact that they are asserting things they do not know, this word may be understood in two ways. *Illusions* are understood by some as though demons present something to the senses that does not actually exist as if it did; for instance, that a house may seem to be there when there is none. But to do this, they either stimulate the brain of the spectator in the way it is usually affected when a house is before them, or they present a certain sort of appearance of a house in the air which strikes the eyes of the spectators. Whichever of these one chooses, it must be shown how this is not a miracle; for both actions are performed beyond human power, outside the order of nature, and at the moment the magician pleases.
> 
> ‘ *Eighthly*, those delusions <note>original: "Opinions"</note> or diseases of the brain which witches have—who believe they go to feasts and dances—when they talk about it to others who have a fearful disposition and weak minds, they bring others into the same fits of madness <note>original: "Fury"</note>. Like a contagion, this spreads far and near, infecting many minds. However, it is observable that these diseases are more frequent among the inhabitants of mountains and isolated places than among those who live in cities or among crowds of people, etc. To this he adds: whoever weighs these things will not wonder if the beliefs of witches are considered illnesses of melancholy <note>original: "melancholly Diseases"; in this period, "melancholy" was a medical diagnosis for various mental disturbances, including depression and hallucinations</note>.’

<header>The Preface to the Reader.</header>

> 'Yet even if these things are so, I will not state that the things which witches report have never actually happened, etc.'

From this, it clearly appears that according to the opinions of the most learned and greatest philosophers, as well as the testimony of divine revelation <note>meaning: the Bible or direct communication from God</note>, there may be such things as spirits which can produce effects beyond the power of human nature.

If it is asked how we define spirits, we may offer what Monsieur Le Clerc says in the preface to his *Pneumatology* <note>original: "Pneumatologia"; a branch of philosophy or theology dealing with spiritual beings</note>, namely:
> 'We call all things endowed with an understanding and a will "spirits." As we consider them spiritual substances, he says he has shown in his *Logic* that the inner nature of any substance, including that of a spirit, is unknown to us. Therefore, people should not expect an absolute, complete description of spirits.'

Based on what we have presented, we do not doubt that sufficient arguments can be drawn to confirm the existence of spirits, supported both by the testimony of Holy Scripture <note>original: "Holy Writ"</note> and by the examples recounted in the following case histories. Just as reason and revelation serve to confirm our belief and pave the way for our trust in historical truths, so do historical truths confirm and provide a foundation for moral demonstration <note>a logical proof based on the weight of evidence and human experience rather than mathematical certainty</note>. Nothing has a stronger claim to truth than histories backed by good authority and reliable witnesses.

We could add many more arguments to prove the existence of spirits and to demonstrate the possibility of such supernatural operations. However, since in this collection <note>original: "History"</note> we have only included those which seem to be authentic, we have chosen instead to provide the

# The Preface to the Reader.

...opinions and arguments of approved and noted writers to prove their possibility, rather than to offer any of our own, which might be less valid because they carry less authority. Even so, reason—no matter what its authority—ought to prevail as long as it carries the probability of truth along with it.

All that we shall add on this occasion, by way of a preface, is this: the purpose <note>original: "Design"</note> of this work is to show what power the Devil has to torment as well as deceive mankind when Almighty God chooses to permit him. It also shows what evil instruments <note>original: "evil Instruments"; referring here to witches or humans who act as agents for the Devil</note> he often uses to cause their own destruction, as well as the harm of those who are tormented by them. 

If this book fulfills its intended purpose and reminds us how much we owe our safety to Divine Providence <note>God's protective care and guidance</note>, and how much we ought to reverence and adore our Great Protector <note>meaning: God</note>, while detesting and loathing the works of the Devil, then we shall be very satisfied. These goals will make our efforts both acceptable and useful to the public.

Nor are the accounts of apparitions, spirits, and other supernatural <note>original: "preternatural"</note> appearances any less indisputable. The truth of such things is testified by the unquestionable evidence of a great many eyewitnesses of integrity <note>original: "Probity"</note> and truth <note>original: "Verity"</note>, who have been struck with terror and amazement at the sight of such spectacles, as well as by the dreadful examples of those wicked persons who have suffered from the attacks <note>original: "Insults"</note> of those evil spirits.

Since the truth of such things is indisputable—being confirmed by the testimony of eyewitnesses and the undoubted authority of both ancient and modern authors—we shall not trouble the reader here with a tedious list of arguments to prove the possibility of such things. This possibility sufficiently appears from the history of those wicked practices, attested both by sacred and other writers, and is also confirmed by the severe punishments inflicted on those who have practiced such unlawful arts, and the dreadful effects produced by their unjustifiable <note>original: "unwarrantable"</note> proceedings. Nor do we need to use stronger or more convincing arguments to prove the possibility of specters and apparitions than the visual proof <note>original: "ocular Demonstration"</note> and testimony of the senses of those who have seen such things and have been terrified by such unwelcome appearances.

(videlicet)</note>, first, we will begin with the most ancient and famous historians of this kind and continue that history down to modern writers. The testimony of historians and the undeniable accounts of facts may be sufficient—and are indeed the best arguments—to prove the reality of the wicked practices and arts used by witches, sorcerers, necromancers <term>those who claim to communicate with the spirits of the dead to predict the future</term>, magicians, and others. ## CHAPTER I.

*Containing a brief account of the diabolical arts of witchcraft, sorcery, and other magical practices, and how the Devil comes to deceive <note>original: "delude"</note> men into practicing them; what contract the Devil makes with them, and the difference between God’s miracles and the Devil’s; the manner in which men involve themselves in such practices; as also the actions of witches and their manner of meeting together, and what worship <note>original: "adoration"</note> they pay to the Devil, their master; as also how they transport themselves from place to place; what their actions are in respect to the people they target <note>original: "work upon"</note>, the extent of their power, and what methods are proper to remedy the harm <note>original: "mischief"</note> done by them; what sort of persons are most subject to be affected by them; why the Devil appears to them in different shapes, and the different kinds of spirits that disturb men or women.*

---

That the art of witchcraft has been practiced in former ages plainly appears from sacred history <note>The author is referring to the Bible as "sacred history."</note>: For when Saul was troubled in

<meta>This page continues the discussion of King Saul’s consultation with the Witch of Endor, using it as a biblical foundation to prove the existence of witchcraft and the Devil’s power to deceive.</meta>

<header>The HISTORY of</header>

spirit, and his heinous <note>original: "heinous," meaning wicked or hateful</note> offenses troubled his conscience, he immediately <note>original: "presently"</note> applied himself to a woman who dealt with a familiar spirit (1 Samuel 28). At his desire, she raised up an unclean spirit which, according to the text, Saul knew to be Samuel. It appeared in his likeness so exactly that Saul took it to be the spirit of Samuel. The Devil is able to transform himself and to appear in different shapes, as 2 Corinthians 11:14 says: "Satan transforms himself into an angel of light." That God permits the Devil, on some occasions, to represent the spirits of men and to foretell things to come is not only apparent in this case, but the same is clear from Micaiah’s prophetic discourse to King Ahab (1 Kings 22).

And that there is such an evil practice as witchcraft, and that witches exist, is apparent from several other places in Scripture, as it is plainly prohibited by the law of God (Exodus 22). The truth of this further appears from the power of Pharaoh's wise men (Exodus 5 and 8), who imitated many of the miracles performed by Moses, which hardened that wicked tyrant's heart. Furthermore, in 1 Samuel 15, Samuel told Saul that disobedience is like the sin of witchcraft. That there were others of the same craft is manifest from the story of Simon Magus (Acts 8) and the woman who had the spirit of Python <note>original: "Spirit of Python," referring to a spirit of divination; the term comes from the Greek *pythōnos*</note> (Acts 16), not to mention several other places in Scripture which mention the practice of witchcraft and such diabolical arts.

As for the means by which the Devil lures <note>original: "allures"</note> men to these wicked practices: since he is the common enemy of man's salvation, he is industrious in prompting them toward such vices as are most agreeable to their temperaments and dispositions. The most heinous impieties are the most pleasant and delightful to those who have forsaken God and are given over into the hands of

...of the Devil, whom he tries to lead so far into his snares that it is impossible for them to get out again. The chief and principal motives by which people are often led into these traps are curiosity in some—who, to please their fancy, sell their souls and bodies to the Devil—while others are moved to it by an uncontrolled thirst for revenge for some injuries they have suffered, or through an overly greedy desire for profit.

And just as there are three inducements to lure people to such unjustifiable practices, so there are two sorts of persons who may be enticed to pursue this art: namely, the learned and the unlearned. There are also two methods of exciting them to this forbidden curiosity: the Devil’s school and his rudiments <note>original: "rudiments," meaning basic principles or early stages of learning; here it refers to folk magic or superstitions</note>. 

The "Devil’s school" is a too-eager desire and pursuit of knowledge and natural causes. When men begin to find themselves skilled to some degree in these areas, and find that they can explain several things through natural causes, they are tempted to advance too far. Where lawful arts and sciences fail to satisfy them, they are likely to apply themselves to the dark and illegal science of magic. Finding that several kinds of circles and conjurations <note>original: "Conjurations," the act of calling upon or summoning a spirit or ghost</note> added to them will raise various kinds of spirits to resolve their doubts, they attribute these effects to the power inherently belonging to the circles and to various words of God, confusedly wrapped together. They flatter themselves that they are masters and can command the Devil, though at the same time they enroll themselves as his slaves and lose Paradise, just as *Adam* did by eating the forbidden fruit. 

The "rudiments" of the Devil are those illegal charms which old women often use to produce effects without natural causes; such as charms to prevent "evil eyes" <note>original: "evil Eyes," a folkloric belief that a gaze from a malicious person could cause bad luck or injury</note>, and so on, by knotting various kinds...

Thus, he allures them to seek a reputation through this deceitful art, or entices them to it through ambition or the hope of gain, and makes a firm contract with them on that account.

Of this kind of unlawful and unjustifiable proceeding is that part of *astrology* which pretends, by the influence of the stars, to foretell which kingdoms will flourish or decay; which persons will be fortunate or unfortunate; which side shall gain the victory in battle; who shall overcome in single combat; how, or at what age, men shall die; or which horse shall win a race—topics which *Cardanus* <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576), an Italian polymath who wrote extensively on astrology and mathematics</note> and *Cornelius Agrippa* <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535), an influential German physician and occult philosopher</note> have written about at length. The prophet *Jeremiah* plainly forbids us to believe or listen to prophecies from those who foretell by the course of the planets and stars (Jeremiah 10).

But the art of *magic* is not only unlawful but dangerous, which soon makes magicians weary of the art they first practice and willing to agree to a deal with the Devil at an easy rate and with less hazard. For in practicing the magical art, not only must certain seasons, days, and hours be used, but circles must also be made—triangular, quadrangular, round, double, or single—according to the form of the apparition that is desired. He uses characters and crosses, drawn both inside and outside the ritual circle and across its borders. The Devil uses various forms of apparitions to deceive the practitioner once the conjured spirit appears. If the magician misses even the smallest of the rites <note>original: "Rights"</note> and ceremonies, or if he becomes frightened by the apparition and steps outside the circle, he forfeits both his soul and body. He is immediately carried away by that spirit, and the Devil takes payment for that debt right away, which he otherwise would have had to wait longer for.

But to proceed to the nature of the contract the Devil makes with them, it consists of either "forms" or "effects." By "forms," I mean the shape he is to take when they call for him. By "effects," I mean the services he agrees to perform for them. These forms and effects become more significant depending on the skill and art of the magician. 

For some, he agrees to appear in the form of a dog, a cat, an ape, or other animals; or he may only answer them with a voice. He may agree to be useful to them in curing diseases or performing some other lowly <note>original: "base"</note> task required of him. To satisfy the most curious practitioners, he often agrees to enter dead bodies and use them to answer questions about future events—such as the outcome of battles or other matters concerning the affairs of kingdoms or commonwealths. For some, he consistently provides his service in the form of a page <note>a young male servant</note>. 

Sometimes he allows himself to be conjured into the form of a tablet <note>a small engraved piece or locket</note> or a ring, which the person he has contracted with can carry around at all times. He empowers some to sell goods which will seem more expensive or at a lower price <note>original: "better cheap"</note> depending on whether the spirit conjured within them lies or tells the truth. And though all devils are, by their very nature, liars, they still deceive their wretched slaves and make them believe that—

When Lucifer fell, some spirits fell into the air, some into the water, some into the fire, and some onto the land, and they still remain in those elements today. They conclude that those who fell into the fire or air are "truer" <note>original: "truer"; in this context, it likely means more powerful or spiritually refined than those in the "grosser" elements of earth and water</note> than those who fell into the water or onto the land. Although by falling from the grace of God they are all of the same status and wander throughout the earth to carry out whatever God permits them to do—and when these executors of God's wrath have finished their work on earth, they are to be enclosed in hell on the Last Day along with those they have deceived—they still try to persuade men that there are princes, dukes, and kings among them. They claim these leaders command smaller or larger legions and hold empire and power over various arts and different regions of the earth.

But to proceed: just as the forms in which the Devil commits himself to appear to the most inquisitive <note>original: "curious"</note> magicians are wonderful, the results <note>original: "Effects"</note> are equally impressive. He commits himself to teaching them arts and sciences, bringing them news from any part of the world, and revealing the secrets of any person, provided they have been spoken aloud. Furthermore, he teaches his students how to win the favor of princes by informing them of various great matters, some of which prove to be true and some false. He can also enable them to please princes with exquisite <note>original: "dainty"</note> entertainments, providing these in a short time from the most remote parts of the world. Sometimes he pretends to guard his students with the mere appearance of armies of cavalry and infantry, as well as castles and forts. These are only illusions, formed by a spirit making impressions on substances in the air. He also teaches them several juggling tricks with cards or dice to enable them to deceive men's senses and other such dishonest practices.

He lures them with attractive promises of great riches and worldly gain. To those who are vengeful, he promises the satisfaction of achieving the ends they desire. Yet the Devil is so crafty that he does not pretend to lure anyone—even those possessed by the passions mentioned above—unless they are encouraged by their lack of judgment, their sinful lifestyle, or their neglect and disdain for God. When he finds them in despair for any of these reasons, he considers it the right time to attack and reveal himself to them. 

Then, either as they walk alone in the fields or while they are in their beds without company, he approaches them either as a voice only or in the shape of a man. He asks what troubles them and promises a certain remedy, provided they follow his advice and what he requires of them. To this they are all too ready to comply, their minds having been prepared beforehand to accept his temptations. Having thus achieved his goal, he immediately appoints another meeting; and before he makes any further proposals, he first persuades them to give themselves up to his service. Then he reveals what he truly is, forces them to renounce their God and their baptism directly, and places his mark <note>The "Devil's Mark" was historically believed to be a physical sign—like a mole or numb patch—left by the Devil to seal his pact with a witch.</note> upon some secret part of their body. 

This mark remains sore and unhealed until their next meeting, and ever after it remains insensitive <note>original: "unsensible"; meaning lacking physical sensation or feeling.</note>, even if it is pinched or poked—which is proven by daily trials <note>original: "daily Trials"; referring to the practice of "pricking," where suspected witches were poked with needles to find spots that did not feel pain or bleed.</note>. This is done to let them know that, as he could both hurt and heal them, all their future misfortunes or happiness must depend on him. Furthermore, the unbearable pain they feel in the place where he marked them serves to distract them and hinders them from resting until their next meeting, lest in the meantime they should forget him, as they are not yet sufficiently confirmed in their new foolishness.

As for their actions, they are of two kinds: namely, in respect to themselves or other persons. Regarding their own actions, so that they may perform the services the Devil employs them for, he mimics in his servants the same kind of service that God requires from those who serve Him.

As for their consultations, these are most commonly held in churches when they meet together for worship <note>original: "adore"</note>. To accomplish the small designs they aim for, he teaches them how to disjoint dead bodies and, by grinding them into powder, mix them with other things that he provides for them.

As for the reason why women are more frequently involved in this craft than men, it is partly caused by their frailty—as Eve was thought to be the more suitable subject for the Devil to work upon <note>This reflects a common early modern belief that women were more susceptible to the Devil's temptations due to the biblical story of the Fall of Man.</note>—and partly because they are more inclined toward revenge.

<meta>This page continues the explanation of how the Devil uses various methods to deceive people and cause harm, following the previous discussion on the use of wax images to waste away a victim's life.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>The HISTORY of [WITCHCRAFT]</header>

[The Devil] knows the nature and properties of medicines better than humans do, and consequently knows how to evade the power of those medicines they make use of. Just as God opened the eyes of the blind using clay and spittle—which could not have had that effect by their own natural properties—so the Devil seeks to deceive his servants by using external means that have no inherent power to perform what is proposed, but are instead brought about in some other way. By such similar means, these servants of the Devil claim to make men and women love or hate; the Devil himself manipulates the corrupted emotions <note>original: "Affections"</note> of those whom God allows him to influence in whatever direction he chooses. In this way, with God's permission, he brought sickness upon *Job*. Since the Devil is a subtle spirit and understands which humors <term>Humours: in early medicine, these were the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed to control health and temperament.</term> prevail within us, he can change their state and make them toxic <note>original: "of a vicious Temper"</note> when God allows him.

Furthermore, it is very likely that they can take away the lives of men or women by roasting "pictures" <note>The practice of burning or melting wax effigies (images) to cause a person's health to waste away.</note>. Although a wax object <note>original: "Instrument of Wax"</note> has no natural power to produce such effects, the Devil, being a subtle spirit, can dissolve the form of the "spirits" <note>In this context, "spirits" refers to the "vital spirits" thought to circulate in the blood and sustain life.</note> and thus prevent them from working together to aid digestion and supply the body with new nourishment while the existing nourishment is scattered and consumed.

Moreover, they can raise storms or tempests in the air, either at sea or on land, if God permits them. These are not universal, but occur only in specific places. They are different from other storms because they are sudden and violent, but do not last as long as natural ones. This is quite possible for the Devil to do since he is a spirit and closely related to the element of air, which a spirit can easily move. We read in Scripture itself that the Devil is given the title *Prince of the Air* <note>A reference to the New Testament, Ephesians 2:2.</note>.

Another power they are said to possess is the ability to make people frantic <note>original: "phrensical"</note> and insane; since these are natural illnesses, the Devil may cause them just as easily as any other disease. They can likewise cause spirits to follow and trouble people, or to haunt certain houses and frighten the inhabitants—actions we have often known witches to perform. Similarly, they can cause some to be possessed by spirits, as demoniacs <term>Demoniacs: individuals believed to be possessed by an evil spirit or demon.</term>.

As for the people whom God allows to be afflicted in this way and placed under the power of witchcraft, they fall into three categories: first, those whom God deems fit to punish for their horrific sins; second, the godly, to awaken them from some great sin or weakness <note>original: "Infirmities"</note>, or to test their patience as Job’s was tested, for it is within God’s power to allow any punishment to be inflicted as He pleases. Just as God may allow some people to be more under their influence than others, those most susceptible to the power of witchcraft are often people of weak or infirm minds who are lacking in faith. Conversely, God will not allow the Devil, their master, to harm those who trust in Him and defy the witches' power through faith.

However, although witches have the powers we have observed—such as transporting themselves from one place to another and accomplishing things with the help of the Devil—once Almighty God sees fit to bring them to justice, and they are imprisoned and confined by the laws of God, the Devil has no further power to help them, as he cannot snatch <note>original: "wrest"</note> them out of God's hands. Even when they are imprisoned and facing punishment for their crimes, the Devil remains ready to deceive them. If they comfort themselves with empty hopes, he encourages their false expectations; or, if he finds them in despair, he is just as ready to

As to the forms the Devil appears in on such occasions, it depends either on their agreement or the nature of the circles and conjurations <term>Conjurations: rituals or incantations used to summon a spirit or devil.</term> they perform. Yet to some, he appears however he pleases, or as he thinks will suit their temperaments <note>original: "Humours"</note>; so that at the same time, he makes himself appear in different forms to several persons, as he thinks will best suit his goals. And although he is a creature made of air <note>original: "airy Body"</note>, he will sometimes so delude the senses as to appear to them like a solid substance; or he inhabits a dead body, which he makes use of as long as he thinks fit.

Thus much being said of magic and witchcraft, we shall, in the next place, proceed to consider the nature of those spirits which appear to and trouble both men and women, frequently frightening them with their dreadful appearances.

These spirits, then, which fright and disturb people frequently by the amazing effects they produce, or by their dismal and astonishing appearances, may be divided into four different kinds: the first are those which trouble houses and solitary places; the second are such as haunt and follow several persons and are often troublesome to them; the third are such as enter into persons and possess them; the fourth sort are such as are commonly called *fairies* <note>original: "Fayries"</note>.

How these spirits have been troublesome to persons by the power of witchcraft has been shown already. We shall, in the next place, say something of their natural appearance without the power of witchcraft; though no doubt they are all one kind of spirit, which only differ in their form of appearance and the manner of their actions.

Regarding the first kind of these spirits, which haunt houses and appear in several horrible forms while making a great noise, they are called *Lemures* <note>original: *Lemures*, a Latin term for the restless spirits of the dead</note> or *Spectra* <note>original: *Spectra*, a Latin term for apparitions or ghosts</note>. They sometimes appear in the form of deceased persons and are called by different names depending on their specific actions. We are assured by the Holy Scriptures that such spirits appear; for example, in the Book of Isaiah <note>original: "Esay"</note>, chapters 13 and 34, the prophet—threatening the destruction of Jerusalem <note>In the Bible, these chapters actually refer to the desolation of Babylon and Edom, though the author attributes them to Jerusalem.</note>—declares that it shall not only be ransacked but become such a desolate place that it shall be the home of owls <note>original: "Howlets"</note>, and of *Ziim* and *Ijim* <note>Hebrew terms often translated as wild beasts, desert creatures, or jackals; here interpreted as demons or spirits</note>, which are Hebrew names for these spirits. They haunt solitary places so they can better terrify people and shake the faith of those who visit such spots. When they haunt houses, it either shows a great deal of ignorance <note>likely referring to spiritual vulnerability</note>, or they are permitted to disturb the neighbors because of their blatant and slanderous sins.

The way they enter these houses varies according to the form they take at the time. If they have inhabited a dead body, they can easily open a door or a window without making any noise. If they enter only as a spirit, any opening where air can pass through is sufficient for them to pass. If it is asked whether the Devil has the same power to enter the bodies of righteous men as he does wicked men, I answer that his entrance into their bodies after the soul has left <note>the author is referring to the possession of corpses</note> cannot defile them. Therefore, there is no harm or disadvantage to the person to whom the body belonged. Nor is it any greater dishonor to them than hanging, beheading, or other shameful deaths that good men frequently suffer. The bodies of both the faithful and the unfaithful are equally free from corruption <note>here meaning spiritual stain or loss of sanctity</note> and worthy of honor until the Last Day, when the bodies of the righteous shall be cleansed and purified.

Although these spirits actually haunt such places, they are not visible to everyone, but only to those whom Almighty God sees fit. In the past, the Devil appeared in the form and shape of people who were dead or about to die to distract and mislead the Gentiles <note>original: "Gentiles," referring here to ancient non-Christian pagans</note> by predicting a person's death, revealing the final will of the deceased, or the manner of their passing. Similarly, he now appears to some ignorant Christians to deceive and mislead them. It is not possible for the souls of the deceased <note>original: "Defunct"</note> to return to Earth, nor would holy angels take on such forms.

Regarding the other two kinds of spirits—those that haunt and trouble people from the outside, or those that possess them from within—these may both be considered the same type because of the similarity of their appearances and actions, as well as the types of people they are permitted to disturb.

The people who are usually troubled by these kinds of spirits are of two types, namely <note>original: "viz.", short for the Latin *videlicet*</note>: either those who are guilty of great offenses, or those whom God permits to be tempted to test their patience, to warn those who watch them, or so that others might have reason to praise God that they themselves are not being punished in such a terrifying way. The Devil's intent and design in tormenting people in this way is either to provoke them to mistrust and blaspheme God because of their unbearable <note>original: "Intolerableness"</note> torments—as he attempted to do with *Job*—or to convince them to make promises to him to persuade him to stop troubling them. And although these types of spirits not only bother and torment some people but sometimes even warn them of upcoming dangers, this is only to deceive humanity. They do this so that people might think themselves safe and trust the one who appears willing to help them, even though all the while he was outwardly appearing to do

As for those who are possessed by these kinds of spirits, although some might think they are only suffering from a frenzy <note>original: "Phrensy," likely meaning delirium or mania</note> or some other illness, those who are truly possessed can easily be distinguished from such people by several symptoms—especially three. I will not mention the trivial signs that the Papists <note>original: "Papists," a contemporary derogatory term for Roman Catholics</note> claim as proof, such as reacting violently to holy water, recoiling from the cross, etc.

The second is the violent lifting of the person's chest and belly, with intense movements and agitations within; the tendons <note>original: "Sinews"</note> become extended and stretched as hard as iron itself.

The Devil deceived the people's senses so much that these spirits appeared to them to actually be doing such things.

## CHAPTER II.

### *Concerning Ghosts and Spirits that walk by night, and about strange noises, cracks, and events that occur before the death of men, great slaughters, and the changing of kingdoms.*

Having given the reader a general overview <note>original: "Notion or Scheme"</note> of the art of magic and witchcraft in the previous chapter—along with the actions of magicians and witches regarding both themselves and others—and having briefly shown the nature and differences of the spirits involved in specters, apparitions, and other supernatural <note>original: "preternatural"</note> occurrences, we shall proceed to specifics in this chapter. We will provide a historical account of witchcraft, magic, apparitions, ghosts, and so on, according to the times and places where they occurred, following the method proposed above.

First, although many people who are depressed <note>original: "melancholy"</note>, insane, fearful, or of weak mind often imagine things to exist that really do not, and are often deceived by other people or animals, and sometimes mistake things resulting from natural causes for monsters <note>original: "Bugbears"</note> and spirits; yet it is most certain and beyond dispute that those things which appear to men and frighten them do not always result from natural causes. Spirits do often appear, producing very strange and wonderful effects. Such things are attested by ancient historians of very good reputation <note>original: "Credit"</note>, who have provided their accounts based on the testimony of people who saw them during both the day and the night.

*Suetonius* <note>A Roman historian (c. 69 – c. 122 AD)</note> tells us that when *Julius Caesar* marched out of *France* <note>The author refers to Gaul as France</note> into *Italy* with his army and came to the River *Rubicon* (which separates *Italy* from southern *France*), he stayed there a while. He was considering the great undertaking <note>original: "Enterprize"</note> he had begun and was doubtful whether he should cross the river or not. Suddenly, a man of impressive height <note>original: "excellent Stature"</note> and handsome appearance appeared to him, sitting nearby and playing a pipe made of a reed. This drew together a great many shepherds and soldiers from the camp, as well as many trumpeters who gathered to hear him. Having collected a great number of people this way, the figure suddenly snatched a trumpet from one of them, leaped into the river, and, sounding the alarm <note>original: "blowing up the Alarum"</note> with a powerful breath, went over to the other side. Upon seeing this, *Caesar* said, "Good luck attends us; let us go where the gods encourage us and where the injustice <note>original: "Iniquity"</note> of the people calls us. The die is cast <note>original: "The Dice are thrown"; Latin: *Alea iacta est*</note>." Immediately following this, he crossed over the river.

To this we shall add what *Plutarch* relates in the *Life of Theseus*: namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, that many who were in the

Often, most grievous groanings were heard, so that ever after, the citizens were obliged to wall up the bath house doors.

Very much amazed and frightened by this astonishing sight, he <note>Dion of Syracuse</note> called his friends and acquaintances to him, telling them what he had seen and asking for their company throughout the night. Because he was extremely shaken by the apparition, he feared it might appear to him again if he were alone—though it never did. However, a few days later, his son threw himself headlong from the roof of the house and died; and Dion himself, after being stabbed through the body, ended his miserable life.

The same author <note>Plutarch</note> tells us, in the *Life of Brutus* <note>original: "Decius Brutus," though Plutarch's subject was Marcus Junius Brutus, the assassin of Julius Caesar</note>, that when *Brutus* decided to move his army out of *Asia* into *Europe*, he was in his tent around midnight. The candle was burning dimly, and the whole army was quiet and silent. As he was deep in thought, he imagined he heard someone entering his tent. Looking back toward the door, he saw the form of a terrible and monstrous body, much larger than the normal height of men, which stood close by him without speaking a word. Although this frightening apparition made him quite afraid, he even ventured to ask this question: *“What are you? A god or a man? And for what purpose have you come to me?”* To which the apparition answered, *“I am, O Brutus, your evil ghost; you shall see me at Philippi <note>original: "Philippus"</note>.”* Then *Brutus*, recovering from his surprise, said, *“I will see you then.”* When this vision had vanished, he called his servants, who told him that they had neither seen any such thing nor heard any voice at all. 

All that night *Brutus* could not sleep at all, but in the morning he went to *Cassius* and told him the whole story of this strange vision. However, *Cassius* looked down on and despised all such things, attributing them to natural causes. But afterward, after *Brutus* was defeated on the battlefield by *Augustus* and *Antony* <note>original: "Anthony"</note> at *Philippi*, he killed himself rather than be delivered into the hands of his enemies.

To this we shall add that *Valerius Maximus* <note>A Roman historian known for compiling a collection of historical anecdotes</note> tells us that *Gaius Cassius* saw *Julius Caesar* at the Battle of *Philippi*. Caesar appeared in a much more majestic form than could belong to any man, spurring his horse and charging with a terrifying and threatening expression. At this sight, *Cassius* turned his back on the enemy and fled, and soon after murdered himself.

*Dio Cassius Nicaeus* <note>A Roman historian of Greek origin who wrote a massive history of Rome</note>, in his *Roman History*, tells us of *Drusus* <note>Nero Claudius Drusus, a Roman general and father of Claudius</note> that after he had ravaged and overrun *Germany* on every side, he came to the River *Abbis* <note>likely the Elbe</note>. Being unable to cross it, he erected monuments to his victories there and turned back; for he met a woman there who exceeded all other mortal creatures in majesty, who said to him: *“Drusus, you who can set no limits to your boundless ambition, where are you going? It is not lawful for you to go any further; therefore, leave this place, for the end of both your life and your great deeds is now at hand.”* Upon hearing this, *Drusus* immediately changed his course; but on his journey, before he reached the River *Rhine* <note>original: "Rein"</note>, he fell sick and died. The same author mentions several other things that foretold his death, though he did not heed them; for instance, two young men appeared on horseback upon the ramparts, and the shrieking of women was also heard, along with several similar omens.

*Pliny the Younger* <note>original: "Plinius Secundus," a lawyer and author in ancient Rome</note>, a citizen of *Como* <note>original: "Novocomensis"</note>, wrote a letter about the appearance of spirits to his friend *Sura*, which we shall consider next. He tells us that *Curtius Rufus*, who was then a companion to the proconsul of *Africa*—being both poor and of low reputation—was walking one day in a gallery toward evening. There, he met an apparition in the shape of a woman, larger and more beautiful than any living creature. While he was amazed at the sight, she told him that she was *Africa* and had come

But what is much more terrible and wonderful is the following account. There was in *Athens* a grand and very large house, but it was reputed to be very unlucky and unfortunate. Around midnight, the strange noise of iron could be heard, and if one listened closely, the rattling of chains. At first, the sound seemed to be far off, but by degrees, it approached nearer and nearer. In a little while, there appeared the apparition of an old man who looked haggard and repulsive, with a long beard and hair standing on end <note>original: "staring hair"</note>. He had fetters on his legs and carried chains in his hands, which he constantly rattled together. Those who lived in the house, terrified by these things, spent many a dismal night staying awake in fear. Afterward, they were afflicted with sickness, and in a short time, as their fear increased, it ended in death. Even in the daytime, though the apparition had vanished, the memory of it was still before their eyes, so that their fear lasted longer than the actual sight of the spirit. Consequently, the house stood empty, deserted, and solitary, being possessed only by the phantom <note>original: "monster"</note> that haunted it; nevertheless, it was offered for sale, if [any...]
