Anyone who was ignorant of its being haunted would have bought it or lived in it. *Athenodorus* <note>Athenodorus Cananites (c. 74 BC – 7 AD) was a Stoic philosopher and a teacher of Caesar Augustus.</note>, by chance coming to *Athens*, read the notice over the door. When he understood the price, thinking it a good bargain, he inquired further. Hearing the truth of the matter, he was all the more eager to hire the house. 

When night came on, he commanded his servants to make his bed in the front part of the house. Having set his writing table and other things in order, he sent all his servants into the inner part of the house. Having done this, he composed his mind and, fixing his eyes upon what was before him, became very intent upon his writing. He did this lest his mind, being unemployed, should be tempted to imagine strange things which might create groundless fears. 

In the beginning of the night there was silence, as in all other places, but not long after, the iron began to ring and the chains began to move. But notwithstanding all that, he would not look up; he continued his writing and covered both his ears. Upon that, the noise increased and drew nearer, sometimes seeming to be outside the porch and sometimes within. At this, he looked back and saw the same things which he had been told of before. 

The apparition stood still and beckoned with his fingers as if he were calling him. The philosopher, on the other side, moved his hand to signify that the ghost should wait a while, and he fell to writing again. At that, the specter shook his chains over his head as he was writing. He looked about and saw him beckoning as before; therefore, he rose up directly and, taking the candle in his hand, followed the apparition, who walked slowly before him as if he were heavily loaded with chains. But as soon as he had turned aside into the courtyard of the house, he suddenly vanished away, leaving the philosopher alone. Finding himself alone, he gathered herbs and

The same author <note>Pliny the Younger (c. 61 – c. 113 AD), a Roman lawyer and author.</note> who relates this story delivers the following account based on his own knowledge, in this manner: He says, "I have a man with me who was once my slave but is now at liberty and free. He is a man not altogether uneducated, and he lay in the same bed as my younger brother. He imagined he saw a certain person sitting on the side of the bed where he lay, putting knives to his head and by that means pulling off his hair. In the daytime, the hair was found upon the ground, and the loss of it was plainly visible around the crown of his head. 

A little time after, the same thing happened again, which confirmed the first report. A boy, among the rest of his schoolfellows, happened to be sleeping in the school; while he was asleep, some people came in through the windows, clothed in white garments, and shaved the hair off his head as he lay there, then went out again the same way they came in. The hair that was shaved off his head, as well as those scattered hairs mentioned before, were found when it was daylight. 

No remarkable accident happened after these things, except that I was not accused of treason—as I would have been if *Domitian* <note>Titus Flavius Domitianus, Roman Emperor from 81 to 96 AD, known for his paranoid and tyrannical rule toward the end of his life.</note> had lived longer. A similar story may be found in a collection of *John Manlius’s* commonplaces <note>A "commonplace book" was a personal collection of anecdotes, quotations, and observations. Johannes Manlius (died 1560) was a German historian.</note>. He tells us that *Theodorus Gaza* <note>Theodorus Gaza (c. 1398–1475) was a Greek humanist and translator.</note> was given a lordship or manor in *Campania* <note>A region in southern Italy.</note> by *Nicholas*, the Pope of *Rome*. One of the farmers on that manor dug up a coffin containing human bones. A spirit suddenly appeared to him, commanding him to bury the coffin again, otherwise his son would die a short time later. When the farmer refused to do so, his son was killed during the night soon after. A few days later, the spirit appeared again to the farmer <note>original: "husbandman"</note>, threatening him that if he did not bury the aforementioned bones, he would kill his other son. The man was shocked by this, and seeing that his other son was falling sick, he related the whole matter to Master *Theodorus*. As soon as *Theodorus* heard it, he went with the man and, digging a grave right by the place where the coffin had been unearthed, they buried the coffin and the bones within it. Immediately afterward, the farmer’s son recovered his health.

*Dio* <note>Cassius Dio (c. 155–235 AD), a Roman historian.</note> tells us that the Emperor *Trajan* was led out of the house where he was staying as a guest during an earthquake and taken into a safer place. This caused him no small amount of trouble and anxiety.

To these accounts, we shall add several histories of the appearance of spirits from very credible authors.

*Alexander ab Alexandria* <note>original: "Alexander ab Alexandria"; Alessandro Alessandri (1461–1523), a famous Neapolitan lawyer and author of "Geniales Dies."</note>, an excellent lawyer born in *Naples*, writes that a certain friend of his—a man of good reputation—was returning toward *Rome* after attending the funeral of an acquaintance. The ghost was very pale and lean, appearing just as he had last been seen on his deathbed.

Instead, it slipped off its clothes and lay down in the same bed, drawing nearer to him as if it wanted to embrace him. And sometimes the whole house seems likely to fall, or a great noise is heard, as if some heavy object fell quite through the house, which, as it appears afterwards, happened at the same time that our friends departed this life. And in some families, before any of them die, there are particular signs and tokens <note>original: "Signs and Tokens"; omens or portents believed to predict future events</note>, such as the doors and windows opening and shutting, or something running up the stairs, or walking up and down the house.

*Cardanus* <note>original: "Cardanus"; Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576), a famous Italian Renaissance polymath who wrote extensively on medicine, mathematics, and the occult.</note> tells us of a noble family of *Parma* in *Italy*, in which, as often as someone died, an old woman was seen in the chimney corner. Once she appeared when a maid of the same family lay sick, which made them despair of her life, but she afterwards recovered; however, another person in the family immediately fell sick and died.

There was a certain parish priest, a very honest and godly man, who during the time of the plague could tell when anyone in his parish was about to die; for in the nighttime he heard a great noise over his bed, as if someone had thrown down a sack of corn from his shoulders. Upon hearing this, he would say, "now another bids me farewell," and the next day he used to inquire who died that night, or who was taken with the plague, so that he might comfort them.

It has often been observed in guildhalls <note>The administrative headquarters of a city or the meeting place of merchant or craft guilds.</note>, where aldermen <note>High-ranking members of a city or borough council.</note> sit, that when one of them has been near death, either a rattling has been heard around his seat or some other sign of death. And the same thing happens regarding pews or stalls

...in churches, or in other places where men have been accustomed to do their work. In country villages, before the death of certain people, either in the evening or in the night, they hear a grave being dug in the churchyard, and the next day they find the grave has been dug. Likewise, in the night when the moon has shone, they have seen some people solemnly walking with a corpse, according to the local custom, or standing before the doors as if someone was about to be carried to church for burial. Often in prisons, when prisoners lie in chains under the sentence of death, a great noise and rumbling is heard in the night, as if someone were breaking into the prison to rescue them; yet the prisoners knew nothing of it, nor can any signs of such an attempt be found afterward. And some executioners or hangmen say that they can usually tell beforehand when any man is to be delivered into their hands to suffer death, for their swords will move on their own accord; and others say they can tell beforehand what deaths they shall die. *Plato* <note>Plato (c. 427–347 BC), the Greek philosopher. This reference is to his work *Laws* (specifically Book IX), where he discusses the restless spirits of the murdered seeking vengeance.</note> tells us in his *Laws* that the souls of those who are slain often cruelly trouble and haunt the souls of those who killed them.

Before the alterations and changes of kingdoms, and in times of wars, seditions, and other dangerous times, very strange things most commonly happen in the air, the earth, and among living creatures—things quite contrary to the course of nature, which are called wonders, signs, monsters <note>original: "Monsters"; in the early modern period, "monsters" or "prodigies" were unnatural events or births believed to be divine warnings of future disasters.</note>, and forewarnings of things to come. Swords and spears, and whole armies of men fighting one another, are often seen in the air, or seen and heard on the land, where one side is forced to flee; and then the most horrific cries are heard, along with the clattering of armor, guns, and lances

<page-num>41</page-num>

And halberds <note>original: "Halberts"; a two-handed pole weapon that combines an axe blade with a spike.</note>, and other kinds of weapons too, often move in armories of their own accord. It is also said that horses will become very dejected and sluggish, and will scarcely allow their masters to sit on their backs, before they go into a battle where they are likely to be defeated. But when they are courageous and spirited, it often signals victory. It is reported by Suetonius <note>Gaius Suetonius Tranquillus (c. 69–122 AD), a Roman historian famous for his biographies of the first twelve Roman emperors.</note> that the horses which Julius Caesar allowed to run free—never to be put to work again—wept when Caesar was murdered.

When Miltiades <note>An Athenian general who led the Greeks at the Battle of Marathon (490 BC).</note> addressed his people against the Persians, terrible noises were heard before the battle. And before the Spartans (Lacedemonians) <note>original: "Lacedemonians"; another name for the inhabitants of Sparta.</note> were overthrown in the Battle of Leuctra <note>original: "Leuctris"; a battle in 371 BC where the Thebans broke the military power of Sparta.</note>, the armor moved and made a great noise in the Temple of Hector. At the same time, the doors of Hercules's Temple at Thebes, though they were securely shut with bars, opened suddenly of their own accord; and the weapons and armor which hung firmly on the wall were found lying on the ground.

In the Second Punic War (the Second War of Carthage), the standard-bearer of the first unit <note>original: "Battalia"; a term for a military formation or battalion.</note> of pikemen could not move his banner out of its place, nor could he do it even when many others came to help him. Although the Consul Gaius Flaminius <note>Gaius Flaminius (died 217 BC) was a Roman leader who famously ignored unfavorable omens before his defeat and death at the hands of Hannibal at the Battle of Lake Trasimene.</note> did not respect this sign, his army was soon routed and he himself was killed. 

In the beginning of the wars fought with the people called the Marsi <note>An Italic people who fought against Rome in the Social War (91–87 BC).</note>, certain voices and the noise of armor <note>original: "Harness"; in this period, the term "harness" often referred to a full suit of armor for a man or horse.</note> were heard coming from hidden places, which foretold the danger of the wars to come. Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman author and naturalist.</note> tells us that in the war with the Danes <note>Pliny likely referred to the Cimbrian War; early modern authors often used the name "Danes" to refer to ancient Germanic or Nordic tribes.</note>, and many times before, the clashing of armor and the sound of trumpets were heard coming from the heavens. And Appian <note>Appian of Alexandria (c. 95–165 AD), a historian who wrote in Greek about the history of Rome.</note> relates what signs and wonders happened before the Roman Civil Wars: what miserable cries of men, clashing of armor, and galloping of horses were heard, even though people could see nothing.

*Valerius Maximus* <note>A first-century Roman author known for his collection of historical anecdotes.</note>, writing of strange wonders, tells us how *Gnaeus Pompey* <note>original: "Cnæius Pompeius"; a leading Roman general and statesman who was Julius Caesar's rival.</note> was forewarned not to fight with *Julius Caesar*. As he set sail from *Dyrrhachium* <note>original: "Dirrachium"; a port city in what is now Albania.</note>, his soldiers <note>original: "Shouldiers"</note> were seized with a sudden fear; and in the night before the battle, they were gripped by terror and their hearts failed them. And *Caesar* himself, in his book *The Civil War*, says that on the same day he fought that fortunate battle, the shouting of the army and the sound of trumpets were heard at *Antioch* in *Syria* so plainly that the whole city ran to their walls in armor to defend them. The same thing happened in *Ptolemais* <note>An ancient port city, now Acre in Israel.</note>.

*Josephus* <note>Flavius Josephus (c. 37–100 AD), a Jewish-Roman historian.</note>, in his history of the wars in *Judea* <note>original: "Jury"</note>, reports the strange signs that happened before the destruction of *Jerusalem*. These included a bronze gate, made fast with iron bars, which opened during the night of its own accord. Also, before the sun set, chariots and armies of men were seen in the air surrounding the city. At *Whitsuntide* <note>original: "Whitsontide"; the Christian festival of Pentecost.</note>, as the priests went into the Temple to celebrate divine service, they heard a great noise, and shortly after a voice crying out, "Let us depart from here." On the same night that *Leo of Constantinople* was slain in the Temple, travelers by the seaside heard the voice of *Leo* even at that great distance.

*Felix Malleolus* <note>Also known as Felix Hemmerlin (c. 1388–1458), a Swiss priest and scholar.</note>, Doctor of Law, Master of *Solothurn* <note>original: "Sclodor"; Hemmerlin was the provost of St. Ursus in Solothurn.</note>, and Canon of *Zurich* <note>original: "Tigurum"; the Roman name for Zurich.</note>—a man of great reading—tells us that in the history of *Rudolf* <note>original: "Rodolphus"; Rudolf I of Germany.</note>, King of the Romans, the said *Rudolf*, having vanquished *Ottokar* <note>original: "Othotarus"; Ottokar II of Bohemia.</note>, King of *Bohemia*, stayed all night in the place where the battle was fought. Around midnight, certain spirits or devils, making a horrible noise and tumult, troubled and disordered his whole army. It was certain that these were spirits walking by night, since they suddenly vanished away like smoke.

And the same author tells us that in the year of our Lord 1280, as one of the *parish priests* <note>original: "Plebans"; from the Latin 'plebanus', a priest in charge of a local parish.</note> belonging to

The same author also claims that in the year of our Lord 1444—before that valiant battle where a small number of Swiss fought against an enormous army belonging to Louis, the Dauphin of France <note>The heir to the French throne, later King Louis XI; this refers to the Battle of St. Jakob an der Birs.</note>, under the walls of Basel <note>original: "Basill"</note> during the time of the General Council—the sounds of soldiers' alarms, the clattering of armor <note>original: "harnesses"</note>, and the noise of men fighting together were heard in those places for several nights.

We are also told of a dignified and wise man, a magistrate in the territory of Zurich, who swore that as he and his servants were walking through the pastures early in the morning, he saw someone he knew very well wickedly engaging in sexual acts with a mare. Amazed by this, he went to the man's house and knocked on the door. He was then assured that the man he thought he had seen had not stepped a single foot out of his bedroom that morning. While the ship was drifting at sea, eventually nearing **Paxi** <note>original: "Paxe"; a small island group south of Corfu.</note>, several people who were awake and others who were drinking after supper suddenly heard a voice calling "**Thamus**," causing great wonder and astonishment among them all.

**Thamus** was an Egyptian-born pilot, unknown to many on the ship. Although he was called twice, he remained silent, but he answered the third time. Upon this, the voice commanded loudly that when he reached **Palodes** <note>original: "Palades"; likely Buthrotum (modern Butrint) on the coast of Epirus.</note>, he should announce that the great god **Pan** was departed <note>This famous story from Plutarch’s *The Obsolescence of Oracles* was traditionally interpreted by Early Modern Christians as a supernatural announcement of the death of Christ, which they believed silenced the pagan oracles.</note>. At this, everyone was struck with fear and amazement, as **Epitherses** <note>The original narrator of the story in Plutarch's work.</note> told us. They consulted whether to obey these commands or not. **Thamus** gave his opinion: if the wind blew, they should pass by in silence; but if it was calm, he would declare what he had heard.

When they arrived at **Palodes**, it was perfectly calm with neither wind nor waves stirring. **Thamus**, looking toward the land, cried out as the voice had instructed: "The great god **Pan** is dead!" He had barely finished speaking when a great groaning from a multitude, mixed with wonder, was heard. As news of this spread, **Thamus** was immediately summoned by the Emperor **Tiberius** to provide an account of this story.

To these stories, we might add several others of a similar kind, such as the chasing or hunting of devils <note>A reference to the "Wild Hunt," a folklore motif of a phantasmal group of hunters in the sky or woods.</note> and the dancing of dead men. These walking spirits sometimes block the paths of men on their journeys and, leading them astray, cause so much terror that some have had their hair turn gray in a single night. An acquaintance of **Ludwig Lavater** <note>original: "Lewes Lavaterus"; a Swiss Reformed theologian (1527–1586) who wrote a highly influential book on ghosts and spirits titled *De Spectris*.</note>, one **John Welling**, was an example of this; not many years ago, he met a walking spirit in the...

To these, we shall add the following account: A certain magistrate within the Liberties <note>An area of jurisdiction outside a city’s walls but subject to its laws.</note> of **Zurich** <note>original: "Tigurine"; from *Tigurum*, the Latin name for the Swiss city of Zurich.</note> was entertaining several friends at breakfast before he began a journey. While they were waiting with him, they thought they heard a knife fall from the upper part of the room, yet they could see nothing. While they were talking together about it, they thought they heard it again. At that time, the magistrate came in, and they told him what had happened. Before they had even finished the story, they heard it fall again. The magistrate, who could scarcely believe it, became a witness to it himself. 

Upon this, he began to urge them that, since a great marriage was to be celebrated in a few days in the same place, they should endeavor to preserve the peace and keep themselves sober, lest by quarreling a murder should happen and make it a bloody marriage. After this, he began his journey. Having finished his business in a few days, he returned toward his castle. However, his horse fell into a river which had suddenly flooded due to rain. After he had struggled for a long time to get out of the water, he died miserably.

From this, it appears that it is no difficult matter for the Devil to appear in various shapes—not only as those who are alive, but also as dead men, or in the form of beasts and birds. He sometimes appears in the likeness of a black dog, a horse, or an owl. By these and several other methods, he brings various things to pass. Because he understands the effects and force of natural things through long and great experience, he uses that knowledge to bring about wonderful things. And as he is a subtle and quick spirit who can readily manipulate objects, so by his quickness and knowledge of the natural world, he may easily deceive the eye—

*Johannes Trithemius* <note>original: "Johannes Tritenhemius"; a German abbot and famous scholar of the occult.</note> tells us that one of the sons of *Simeon* <note>original: "Simon"; likely referring to Tsar Simeon I of Bulgaria, who was educated in Constantinople and lived as a monk before taking the throne.</note>, a monk who ruled over the *Bulgarians* and was named *Baianus*, was seen practicing the art of necromancy. He could transform himself into a wolf as often as he pleased, or take the likeness of another beast, or make himself invisible to anyone. The same author also tells us that in the year 876, a certain *Jewish* man named *Sedechias*—at one time the physician and philosopher to Emperor *Louis* <note>original: "Lewes"; likely Louis the German.</note>—was so skilled in sorcery that he could visibly devour an armed man and his horse with all its gear, or a cart loaded with hay along with the horse and driver. He also cut off men’s heads, hands, and feet, which he placed in a basin before the spectators with the blood clearly running around the basin; he would then immediately reattach them to their proper places without any harm to the people. He would also engage in exercise, hunting, and running in the air and clouds, just as men do upon the earth.

Upon this, the conjurer—along with the Duke—entered into his conjuring circle at a most convenient hour and called the spirit that was accustomed to obey his commands. To this the spirit answered, "If the Duke will come with me, I will obey your commands."

Then the spirit flew away and, taking upon himself the form of a pilgrim, he entered the prison where the Duke was kept prisoner and told him, "If you want to be delivered out of captivity, mount this horse immediately, and I will bring you safe and sound without any harm into Austria to your brother, Duke Leopold." To this, the Duke said, "Who are you?" The spirit answered, "Do not ask who I am, for that is nothing to the purpose; just get up on the horse, and I will bring you safely into Austria." 

Upon hearing this, the Duke was seized with horror and fear; he blessed himself with the sign of the cross, and the spirit immediately vanished away with the black horse and returned empty-handed to the one who sent him, telling him the reason why he did not bring the prisoner. When Duke Frederick was at last delivered out of prison by other means, he confessed that what had happened during his imprisonment occurred on the same day they mentioned. This history is to be seen in the *Chronicles of the Swiss* <note>original: "Helvetians"</note>.

## CHAPTER III.

*Containing the most strange and extraordinary <note>original: "admirable"; in this period, "admirable" meant "astonishing" or "to be wondered at" rather than "praiseworthy."</note> discovery of the Three Witches of Warboys, who were arraigned, convicted, and executed at an Assizes <term>Assizes were periodic courts held in English counties for the trial of civil and criminal cases.</term> at Huntingdon for the bewitching of five daughters of Robert Throckmorton, Esq., and various other persons with sundry devilish and grievous torments; and also the bewitching to death of Lady Cromwell; the likes of which have not been heard of in that age.*

Having given the reader an account in the previous chapter—according to our proposed method—of the appearance of ghosts, spirits, and apparitions, we shall next provide a historical account of true and specific observations regarding a notable instance of witchcraft. This was practiced by John Samuel (the father), Alice Samuel (the mother), and Agnes Samuel (the daughter), of Warboys in the County of Huntingdon, upon five daughters of Robert Throckmorton, Esq. (of the same town and county), and other maidservants. In total, twelve people belonging to one house were affected, beginning in November 1589.

About the tenth of November in the year 1589, Mistress Jane, one of the daughters of Mr. Throckmorton, being nearly ten years of age,

Sometimes she would shake one leg and no other part of her body, as if she had palsy <term>Palsy: a term historically used for various types of paralysis or involuntary tremors.</term> in it, and sometimes the other leg. Soon after, she would shake one of her arms, then the other, and shortly after that her head, as if she were affected by "running palsy," continuing in this condition for two or three days. Among other neighbors in the town, *Alice Samuel*, who lived next door on the north side, came into Mr. *Throckmorton’s* house to visit the child.

When the old woman came into the house, the child was being held in another woman’s arms by the fireside. So she went into the chimney corner and sat down close to the child, while the child's grandmother and mother were both present. She had not been there long before the child became somewhat worse than before she arrived. Suddenly, the child cried out while pointing to the aforementioned Mother Samuel, "Did you ever see anyone who looks more like a witch than she does? Take off her black thrummed cap <term>A "thrummed cap" was a hat made of thrums—the coarse, shaggy ends of a weaver's warp threads, creating a fuzzy or fringed texture.</term>, for I cannot bear <note>original: "abide"</note> to look at her!"

The child's mother, suspecting no such thing, was very angry with her daughter and scolded <note>original: "reproved"</note> her for saying so. She thought the outburst might be caused by some mental confusion <note>original: "Lightness in the Child’s Brain"</note> brought on by such violent sneezing and a lack of rest. Therefore, she took her and laid her down on a bed and hung curtains over the windows, hoping that by that means she might be inclined to rest. However, it was only with great difficulty that she was able to calm <note>original: "pacifie"</note> the child.

The old woman, hearing this, sat still without saying a word, yet she looked very miserable <note>original: "dismally"</note>, as those who saw her remembered very well. The child continued in the same condition, getting worse rather than better. Within two days, her parents sent the child's urine to *Cambridge* to Dr. *Barrow*, a very skillful physician. He returned this answer, namely <note>original: "viz." (Latin: videlicet)</note>: that he could perceive no illness <note>original: "distemper"</note>, though he thought she might be troubled with worms. He sent medicines accordingly, but the child did not improve. 

In two days' time, they sent to the same man again, describing her sickness in more detail. He then told them that the urine they had brought showed no sign of such a disorder <note>original: "disquiet"</note> in her body, and he guaranteed that she did not have epilepsy <term>Falling sickness: an archaic term for epilepsy, characterized by seizures and loss of consciousness.</term>, which her parents suspected. He then sent other medicines meant to purge her system, but they did not have the expected effects. Because of this, they sent to him a third time and told him that the medicines had no effect and the child was no better. 

The doctor then looked at the urine again. Perceiving the child's body to be in good health <note>original: "temper"</note>—at least as far as he could see to the contrary—he asked if they suspected any sorcery or witchcraft regarding the child. They answered, "No." At this, he declared it was impossible for the illness to be caused by any natural means without any signs appearing in the urine. Nevertheless, he suggested they send to any other skillful man in the town for their own peace of mind <note>original: "satisfaction"</note>. 

The messenger then went to Mr. *Butler*. After examining the urine and hearing the details of the child's illness <note>original: "distemper"</note>, he said he thought it might be worms—even though he did not detect them in the urine. He thought it strange that worms would cause such symptoms, but he ordered the same medicines described before, though they were not used:

Dr. *Barrow* had previously advised them not to use any more medicines, suspecting that the illness <note>original: "Distemper"</note> was caused by witchcraft. Yet, although Mr. *Throckmorton* and his wife resolved to trust in Almighty God's will, they did not suspect witchcraft until about a month later. At that time, two more of his daughters, who were about two or three years older than the first, fell into the same extreme states as their sister. They cried out against *Mother Samuel*, saying, "Take her away! Look where she stands there before us in a black thrummed cap <note>The OCR reads "thumb'd," but "thrummed" refers to a cap made of tufted or fringed yarn, which was common attire for the elderly or poor in the 16th century.</note>!" (which she commonly wore, though she was not wearing it then). They cried, "It is she who has bewitched us, and she will kill us if you do not take her away!"

This moved their parents to suspect witchcraft, yet they could not imagine why it should be worked against them or their children. They had only moved to town the previous Michaelmas <note>The feast of St. Michael, September 29; a common date in the English calendar for beginning new leases or moving house.</note> and had given no one any cause for malice. About a month later, another sister, younger than the rest and about nine years old, was seized with the same malady and cried out against *Mother Samuel*, just as the others had done.

Soon after, Mrs. *Joan*, the eldest daughter, who was about fifteen years of age, fell into the same condition, but she was handled more severely than the rest. Because she was stronger than the others and struggled more against the spirit, she was unable to overcome it and was more grievously tormented. It caused her to sneeze, shriek, and groan most fearfully. Sometimes it would heave up her belly and jerk <note>original: "bounce"</note> her body up with so much violence that if she had not been held down on her bed, she would have been extremely bruised. Several times, when she had her fit while sitting in a chair, her starting and heaving almost broke the chair she sat in. Yet the more they strove to help them and to keep them down, the more violently they were handled, being de-

These fits would hold them sometimes for a longer and sometimes for a shorter duration—sometimes an hour or two, and sometimes half a day or a whole day. Sometimes they would have six or seven fits in a single hour. Yet, when they came out of them, they knew neither what they had said nor what they had suffered.

When Mrs. *Joan* had been treated this way for a while, the spirit would whisper something in her ear which she would then declare during her fit. On one occasion, it particularly showed her that twelve people would be bewitched in that house, naming them to her. These were all women and servants in the house, herself and her sisters being five of that number. This afterwards proved very true, for all of them suffered their own various afflictions in the same manner as the five sisters.

The servants, when they first fell into fits, all cried out against *Mother Samuel* just as the children did, saying, "*Take her away, Mistress; for God’s sake, take her away and burn her, for she will kill us all if you let her alone.*" They underwent the same miseries and extremes that the others did. When they were out of their fits, they knew no more of what they had said or done than the children did. As soon as these servants left Mrs. *Throckmorton’s* house, they were all immediately well, as they had been before, and they continued so without any further suspicion of such torments <note>original: "Vexations"</note>. However, the servants who were hired to take their places were afflicted in much the same manner for nearly two years.

Mr. *Pickering*, the uncle of the aforementioned children, heard how strangely they were being troubled and went to *Warboys* to see them and visit their parents. Upon arriving at the house where they lived, he found them as healthy as any children could be. 

About half an hour later, Mr. *Pickering* was informed that Mrs. *Audley* <note>original: "Hadley"—the text later refers to her as Mrs. Audley</note>, Mr. *Whittle* of *St. Ives*, and others had gone to Mother *Samuel's* house to persuade her to come and visit the children. However, because she stayed away so long, Mr. *Pickering* concluded that she would not come, even though she had promised she would visit whenever the parents sent for her; she had even claimed she would venture up to her chin in water and lose some of her best blood to help them. 

But now her mind, it seemed, was altered. She said this was because all the children cried out against her and said that she had bewitched them. She also feared that the common practice of *scratching* <term>the folk practice of scratching a suspected witch's skin until it bled, which was believed to break their spell over a victim</term> would be used on her. Indeed, this had been intended, but both the parents and Mr. *Pickering* had sought advice from respected clergymen <note>original: "good Divines"</note> regarding the lawfulness of the practice, and they were told it was wrong. 

Therefore, Mr. *Pickering* went to Mother *Samuel's* house both to see her and to persuade her that if she was the cause of the children's trouble, she should make amends. When he arrived at the house, he found Mr. *Whittle*, Mrs. *Audley*, and others trying to persuade her, but she refused. At that point, Mr. *Pickering* told her that he had the authority to bring her, and if she would not go willingly, he would force her. He did exactly that, bringing her along with her daughter *Agnes* and one *Cicely Burder*, who were <unclear/> all suspected of being witches or of conspiring with Mother *Samuel*.

As they were going to Mr. *Throckmorton's* house, Mr. *Whittle*, Mrs. *Audley*, and the others walked ahead; Mother *Samuel*, *Agnes Samuel*, and *Cicely Burder* walked in the middle; and Mr. *Pickering* followed behind.

<summary>Mr. Pickering, the children's uncle, forcibly brings Mother Samuel and two other suspects to the Throckmorton home after Mother Samuel refused to come willingly due to fears of being "scratched." Mr. Pickering noticed that Mother Samuel very much wanted <note>original: "would fain"</note> to talk with her daughter, Agnes, but he followed so closely that they had no opportunity to speak in private. When they reached Mr. Throckmorton’s door, Mother Samuel curtsied to Mr. Pickering, offering to let him enter before her. This was a trick to get a chance to speak with her daughter in the entryway <note>original: "the Entry"</note>, but he refused to go ahead. At that point, she leaned her head as close as she could to her daughter’s head and whispered, *"I command you <note>original: "I charge thee"</note>, do not confess anything."* 

Mr. Pickering, who was standing right behind them, saw this and thrust his own head between theirs just as she was speaking. Having heard her clearly, he immediately replied to old Mother Samuel, *"Are you commanding your daughter not to confess?"* 

To this she answered, *"I did not say that; I only told her to hurry home to get her father’s dinner ready."* 

While these words were being spoken, Mr. Whittle, Mrs. Audley, and the others went into the house. Three of the children were standing in the hall by the fire, appearing perfectly healthy. However, as soon as Mother Samuel entered the hall, these three children collapsed at the same moment. They were strangely tormented; if they had been left alone, they would have leaped and flopped about like fish recently pulled out of the water. Their bellies arched upward while their heads and heels remained on the ground; they would pull their heads and heels backward toward each other, flinging out their arms and letting out great groans that were terrifying and distressing to those watching. 

Mr. Whittle quickly picked up one of the children—Jane Throckmorton—and carried her to an inner chamber to lay her on a bed. Although he was as strong a man as almost any in England and the child was only nine years old, he could not hold her down on the bed. Her belly would heave up as high as that of a woman who was heavily pregnant <note>original: "big with child"</note>.

Oh, that I had her!"* 

Mr. Pickering wondered at this, as he thought that scratching <note>It was a common folk belief that "scratching" a witch—drawing her blood—would break her spell over the victim. However, legal and religious authorities often viewed this as an unlawful or superstitious practice.</note> was altogether unlawful. Her face was turned away from Mr. Pickering and her eyes were shut. Mr. Whittle lay with almost his whole weight upon her to hold down her belly, fearing that she would otherwise have broken her back.

Despite this "offer" made by the child—or the spirit within her—to reveal some secret by which witches might be discovered, Mr. Pickering went into the hall and took Mother Samuel by the hand. She went very much against her will. He brought her to the side of the bed furthest from the child, who still lay scraping at the bedclothes and crying, *"Oh, that I had her!"* 

Then Mr. Pickering, speaking very softly so the child would not hear, asked Mother Samuel to put her hand against the child’s hand, which she refused to do. At this, Mr. Pickering put his hand to the child’s, as did Mrs. Audley and others, but the child would scratch none of them. Then Mr. Pickering took Mother Samuel’s hand and thrust it against the child’s hand. As soon as she felt it, she scratched with such violence <note>original: "Vehemence"</note> that she splintered her fingernails in her eager de- <note>The catchword "fire" is a transcription of the original "ſire," the second half of the word "desire."</note>

...fire of revenge. While the child was scratching in this way, Mr. Pickering covered Mother Samuel’s <note>Alice Samuel, known as "Mother Samuel," was an elderly woman from Warboys accused of bewitching the five daughters of Robert Throckmorton.</note> hand with his own to see what the child would do in this extreme state of agitation <note>original: "extream Passion"</note>. However, the child would not scratch his hand, but felt back and forth for the hand she missed. If she could touch even one finger of Mother Samuel’s hand, she would scratch that hand and no other. In fact, sometimes when Mr. Pickering covered Mother Samuel’s hand, the child would place one of her own fingers between his and scratch Mother Samuel’s hand with that finger, while the rest of her fingers lay unmoved upon his hand.

And if at any time Mother Samuel’s hand was moved away from the child, she would mourn and show clear signs of distress. This is a fact to be noted as most certain, as Mr. Pickering gave it as evidence at the Huntington Assizes <note>The Assizes were periodic courts held around England; the "Witches of Warboys" trial took place in Huntingdon in 1593.</note>. During this, the child’s eyes were shut so that she could see nothing; even if her eyes had been open, her face was turned the other way and covered so much by Mr. Whittle that it was impossible for her to see anyone on the other side of the bed.

When this was finished, Mr. Pickering went out of this room into another parlor where a woman was holding one of the other children. As Mr. Pickering passed by, this child was clawing at the apron of the woman holding her, crying out, *“Oh! If only I had her! Oh! If only I had her!”* Then Mr. Pickering went to the hall and brought Cicily Burder <note>Cicily was the daughter of Alice Samuel; she and her father John were also accused of witchcraft.</note>, and did the same with her as he had with Mother Samuel for the other child. Just as the first child had acted toward Mother Samuel, so did this child act toward Cicily Burder in every respect.

The third child, who was in the hall, spoke the same words; however, the children's father and Dr. Dorrington, the parson <note>The parish priest or minister.</note> of the parish, would not allow the same experiment to be performed upon the third child.

That same night, after supper, while the children were currently free from their fits and feeling well, Dr. Dorrington suggested <note>original: "moved"</note> having prayers before the company departed. As the company knelt down and he began to pray, all the children immediately fell into their fits with such terrible shrieks and strange sneezings. They were so strangely tormented that it seemed as if they would be torn to pieces. Because of this, Dr. Dorrington stopped in the middle of his prayers and asked, “Had we best go any further?” 

But as soon as he stopped praying, the children became quiet, though they remained in their fits. Then he began to pray again, and at that same moment the fits returned with the same violence, as if the wicked spirit within them was provoked <note>original: "moved"</note>. This was tried many times; as soon as he stopped praying they were quiet, and whenever he began to pray, they began to shriek.

The next day, being Valentine’s Day, Mr. Pickering brought Elizabeth Throckmorton home to his own house at Tichmarsh Grove <note>original: "Tichmerch Grove"</note>. At first, she was in the middle of a fit, but she was no sooner on horseback and out of the town of Warboys <note>original: "Warboyse"</note> than she recovered. She remained well until she arrived at Mr. Pickering’s house; as soon as she arrived, she was suddenly seized with a fit, gasping and unable to speak. 

The fit began in the following manner: first, she threw herself backward, with all the joints of her back pulling together <note>original: "contracted"</note> and her belly thrusting out so violently that no one could bend her back again. She felt very strong and heavy, shaking her limbs and often her head, but especially her arms—much like those having convulsions before death. She was simultaneously dumb, deaf, and blind, with her eyes closed tight. This fit lasted no more than fifteen minutes <note>original: "a Quarter of an Hour"</note> before she came to her senses with a gasp, rubbing <note>original: "stroaking"</note> her eyes as if she had been asleep.

Sometimes during a fit she is only deaf. When she can speak—or rather, as we believe, the spirit within her speaks—she talks very nonsensically <note>original: "vainly"</note>. Although she can see, it is only with a faint glimmer <note>original: "Glimmering"</note>, so that if you were to look at her, you would think she could not see at all.

Sometimes she can only hear, yet not everyone, but only the person she likes best. Sometimes she can only see, as clearly as anyone else, but can neither hear nor speak because her teeth are clenched <note>original: "set in her Head"</note>. Sometimes she can both hear and see but cannot speak at all.

Above all things, she delights in games, choosing <note>original: "chusing"</note> one particular person to play cards with her. However, she chooses only one person and neither hears, sees, nor speaks to anyone else. When she is awake, she does not remember what she did, heard, or said, insisting that she was not sick but only asleep.

She remained well until night. Before supper, during the saying of grace <note>original: "at Thanksgiving"</note>, she was strangely troubled by the very word *Grace*, which affected her only as long as grace was being said. She sat well at the table, but she had no sooner put away her knife than it threw <note>original: "pitched"</note> her backward. After being taken from the table, she was well until the next prayer <note>original: "Thanksgiving"</note>, at which time she was very much tormented, but no longer. Afterward, she was very quiet until they moved to go to prayers. During the entire time of prayer, she seemed as if she would be torn to pieces with such shrieking, outcries, and violent sneezing that she terrified the whole company; but when prayers were ended, she remained quiet.

Mr. *Pickering* and others, observing this, said that if they should read the Bible or any other godly book, it would put her into a rage as before for as long as they read. To test this, one person took a Bible and read the first chapter of *Saint John*, the first verse. At the reading of this, she became like one with a frantic mind <note>original: "distracted in Mind"</note>, but she was quiet

whenever they stopped reading; and when they began to read again, she was tormented once more. This cycle happened several times, ending each time they stopped reading.

In fact, at the mention <note>original: "Motion"</note> of any holy word, such as *God*, or *Pray God bless her*, or whenever anything was named that related to God or piety <note>original: "Godliness"</note>, she raged just as if someone were reading or praying beside her. She was carried to bed, still gripped by her fit.

On Sunday <note>original: "Sabbath-Day"</note> morning, she came down into the hall around the time for prayers. When she was asked whether she would stay during the prayers or not, she answered that she would do as they wished. When asked if she could read, she answered that she once could, but had almost forgotten how now. When asked if she had prayed that day, she answered, "It would not allow me." When asked further if she used to pray at home, she answered that "It" <note>The "it" refers to the perceived demonic presence or the affliction itself.</note> would not give her enough time. 

Upon hearing this, someone said, "Since it will not let you pray, or let anyone else pray for you, pray to yourself secretly in your heart and spirit." As they began to tell her that God understood the inward sighs and groans of the heart as well as the loudest cries of the mouth, she suddenly fell into a fit, being more violently and strangely tormented than ever before. After she was carried away, her fit continued and grew worse throughout the entire prayer time, even though she was out of earshot. She made such vehement cries, screeching, and continual sneezing that those present were forced to stop the prayers several times. The whole company was amazed. When the prayers ended, she came to herself with a gasp, wiping her eyes; she was immediately as well as anyone else, looking as if she had not been distressed <note>original: "disorder'd"</note> at all.

She came down to dinner, and while grace was being said, the fit seized her again. She could tolerate someone else saying grace better than saying it herself, though she could not tolerate anyone doing it well. At dinner-time—

# WITCHCRAFT, &c.

She was reasonably well; and sometimes she had "merry fits," during which she would put her hands away from her food and her food away from her mouth—mocking her and making her miss her mouth. She would sometimes smile and sometimes laugh excessively at this. What was remarkable was that, during her fit, she looked much sweeter and more cheerful than when she was awake; and though she was violently tormented during these episodes, she was as healthy as anyone else when the fit ended. 

Finally, Mr. *Pickering* remembered what had happened at *Warboys* <note>original: "Warboyse"; This refers to the famous 1593 trial of the Witches of Warboys, a landmark case in English legal history involving the Throckmorton children.</note>, namely <note>original: "viz."</note> that if a child were carried into the churchyard during a fit, they would immediately come out of it. However, as soon as they were brought back into the house, the fit would return, but they would recover immediately upon being taken out again. To test this with this child, he carried her out of the house, and she immediately recovered; but upon her return into the house, the fit returned as before. However, the success of this experiment lasted no more than three days.

We rejoiced, however, at these welcome intervals and concluded that since the Devil was only a servant <note>original: "Vassal"</note> used for the wicked purpose of the woman who controlled <note>original: "detain'd"</note> him, the wicked spirit had no power to torment her outdoors. For just as the Angel of God said to *Lot*, "*I can do nothing until you arrive there*" <note>original: "I can do nothing till thou come hither"; Genesis 19:22</note>, even less can devils exceed their assigned authority <note>original: "Commission"</note>. But this experiment soon failed; after that time, when she was carried outdoors, you would have thought she was going to be torn to pieces, much to the surprise and amazement of those who saw her.

From the sixteenth day of *February* to the 26th, she was afflicted most commonly five or six times a day; sometimes ten times, and sometimes only once or twice, and not until night. On the seventeenth of *February*, she could not stop herself from gasping and gaping; and when she was asked the reason,

It was a sign of the fit leaving her when she stretched her arms and gaped frequently and long, with a little rubbing of her eyes; then this continual gaping ceased.

On the 26th of February, she read and sang Psalms, being well all day until evening. When the fit seized her, she cried out against *Mother Samuel* <note>Alice Samuel, an elderly neighbor accused of bewitching the Throckmorton children in the famous Warboys trial of 1593.</note>, fearing as she cried that the woman would put a mouse into her mouth; sometimes a cat or a frog, and sometimes a toad, clapping her hands before her mouth. Being suddenly seized with this fear, she would start out of the hands of those who held her and run away out of doors into another room. There, her legs suddenly failed her, and she was caught by someone following her, still crying, *"Away with your mouse, Mother Samuel, I will have none of your mouse!"* After this time, she imagined she had a mouse in her belly.

The 27th of February she was pretty well, yet in her fit all day long; though awake, she nodded at every second word as if she were drowsy, often with food <note>original: "Meat"</note> in her mouth. Whatever she did, she gave a nod with her head, very low, every minute. This drowsy fit continued nearly two days.

On the first day of March, after dinner, she was seized with such violent sneezing—forty times in succession and faster than one could easily count—that blood came out of her nose and mouth. But all night long while in bed, she fell into the most sorrowful fit of all, weeping with such sorrowful sobs and sighs and crying out so much that no one could comfort her. She cried that now the witches would kill her father and destroy her and all her sisters; this continued for over two hours. Afterward, while sleeping, she suffered a severe nosebleed <note>original: "sad Bleeding"</note>, losing at least a pint from both nostrils and her mouth; but in the morning, she remembered nothing of her fit or the bleeding.

On the second of March, all her fits were merry and full of excessive laughter. They were so hearty and extreme that, had she been awake, she would have been ashamed of being so full of silly nonsense <note>original: "trifling Toys"</note> and merry jokes of her own making, which caused her as well as the bystanders to laugh at them. In this fit, she chose one of her uncles to play cards with her; and, desiring to see the end of the game, they played together. Soon after, a book <note>In cases of suspected possession, "the Book" almost always refers to the Bible or a prayer book, which were believed to cause violent reactions in the afflicted.</note> was brought and laid before her, at which she threw herself backward; but once that was taken away, she immediately recovered and played again. On the fifth of March, she fell into a fit in the morning and expressed a longing to go home to her father’s house. On the sixth, one of her father’s servants came over to Titchmarsh Grove; she had often called for this man while in her fits to carry her to Warboys, to her father’s, saying that if she were even halfway there, she knew she would be well. To test this, they carried her toward Warboys on horseback. Having barely gone the distance of a bow-shot <note>original: "a Bow-shot"; about 200–300 yards</note> to the side of a pond, she woke up, wondering where she was and knowing nothing of what had happened. However, no sooner was the horse's head turned back toward the house than she fell into her fit again. For three days after, and no longer, she woke up and was well whenever she was carried to the pond; but as soon as she turned back again, her fit returned.

On the eighth day of March, she developed a new, strange behavior <note>original: "antick Trick"</note>; she could walk well enough for two steps, but on the third, she came to a complete halt, bowing her head as low as her knees. As she was sitting by the fire, she would suddenly start up, saying she would go to Warboys; but she was stopped at the door. As she was going out, her head nodded and she hit her forehead against the door latch, which raised a lump as big as a walnut. When she was carried to the pond and woke up there, she asked how she had come to be hurt. 

She remained well there all day, playing bowls <note>a lawn game involving rolling weighted balls</note> or some other sport with the other children; for the sillier the games she played, the less she was tormented by the spirit. But as soon as any move was made toward coming into the house, the fit immediately seized her. Consequently, for twelve days, she was never out of her fit while indoors; she ate and drank while in this state, but she could neither see, hear, nor understand, and she had no memory or ability to speak.

On the *ninth of March*, she could not walk but only hop; one of her legs was drawn up so high that it would not touch the ground by a foot. Even when she was carried to the pond, she did not wake up, yet her leg was restored to its normal position. This continued for three more days, though she could only walk on one leg while inside the house.

On the *tenth*, after drinking some milk, she listened intently, as she usually did, asking if anyone else heard the spirit in her belly lapping up the milk she had eaten. Then she began to show a dislike for all bad things and took delight in reading, saying that the spirit loved no goodness. Therefore, she burned all the playing cards she could find. She would try to read even when you would not have thought she could see the book; however, sometimes her eyes were squeezed completely shut, sometimes her tongue was tied, and sometimes her teeth were clenched. Occasionally, she would fling the book away, especially when she reached any "good word" <note>likely referring to the name of God or Jesus</note>. If she could catch the book and hold it, she would strive to do so, pressing it to her face until she could see the words. Sometimes, as she was reading, the spirit would fling her backward and cause her belly to swell in such a strange manner that two strong men were not able to hold her down.

On the *eleventh*, someone asked her if she loved the Word of God; at this, she was greatly troubled and tormented. When they asked, "Do you love *witchcraft*?" she was content. "Do you love the Bible?" it made her shake. "Do you love *Papistry*?" <note>original: "Papistry"; a derogatory term for Roman Catholicism</note>—the devil within her was quiet. "Do you love prayer?" it raged. "Do you love the *Mass*?" <note>The central act of worship in the Catholic Church, which was illegal in England at this time</note>—it was still. "Do you love the *Gospel*?"—it caused her belly to heave. It was clear that the spirit disliked every good thing but was pleased by anything concerning "Popish idolatry" <note>A common Protestant label for Catholic practices like the veneration of saints or the use of images</note>.

And here we should observe that during the entire time *Elizabeth Throckmorton* stayed at *Titchmarsh Grove*, every month from *March* to *July*, she was troubled with some disorder of the body called "fits." She was never entirely free from her first <term>visitation</term> <note>original: "visitation"; a historical term for a period of affliction, illness, or divine/demonic punishment</note>, though in some months she had only one fit.

On the 29th of *July*, she had a fit from noon until night, sleeping most of the time. On the 30th, she had three separate fits in the afternoon, going to bed during each one; yet all of them were mild, without the violent thrashing or excessive sneezing she experienced in her earlier fits.

On the second of *August*, she fell very suddenly after dinner into her usual fits. She did not even have enough time to say, "*It comes*," which were the words she usually used to announce the approach of a fit. This fit was very strong and troublesome; yet towards the end, she fell into a sleep and continued in it until supper time. However, by the next morning all her pain had ceased, and she began to eat her <note>original: "meat"; in the 16th century, this referred to food in general, not just animal flesh</note> cheerfully and was as well as she ever was.

So, she sat in a chair all day and ate her food <note>original: "meat"; in the 16th century, this word referred to food in general, not just animal flesh</note> at the proper times when it was brought to her. However, she would lift her hand as a sign of thanksgiving after eating, which was comforting to those watching her.

On the fourteenth of August, she was carried outside into the open air, but it caused no change in her. Now, however, she began to complain about the side of her body where the leg was drawn up. If anyone touched her on that side, she would whimper and groan as if it were sore, though there was no sign of any external injury. If you touched her on the other side, she would laugh in a joking manner and look happy, yet she did not speak a word all day. From this time until the eighth of September, this drowsy fit continued for a whole month. 

Several noteworthy things happened during this time. Sometimes she would sew all day long and grieve if her work was taken away from her. Other times she would wind yarn or knit, but she never looked up or changed her expression. Some days she would be cheerful and lighthearted, finding many things to delight in, such as playing light and childish games with her cousins, like children.

Yet if they pointed to the place with a finger or gave some other sign, she would turn back and read it over again, sometimes reading it correctly and sometimes not. When she came to the word *Satan* or the *Devil*, she had great difficulty <note>original: "had much ado"</note> passing over it quietly or keeping the book in her hands; for it would shake her arms and strain her body so much that she would often say, *“Will you not allow me to say my prayers? She would by no means give up her book unless the spirit cast it out of her hands by great force and violence. She was usually treated this way <note>The "spirit" or "devil" supposedly possessing her would interfere in this manner.</note> when she went to bed and during times of prayer.

Furthermore, for two or three days, if *Satan* or the *Devil* had been named to her, it would have troubled her; as often as those names were used, she would have a series of twitches, which was very strange to the beholders. At the naming of *Mother Samuel* <note>Alice Samuel, the elderly neighbor accused by the children of being the witch responsible for their fits.</note>, it would shake her by the shoulders and arms as if it would shiver her into pieces, giving signs of great disgust at the name. Sometimes the mere mention of her name would cast Elizabeth into her fit, in the midst of which she would say, *“Could you not have held your tongue? I was well enough before you named her.”*

When this fit ended, her teeth were clenched so tightly <note>original: "her teeth were set in her head"</note> that she lost her ability to speak. After this, she groaned inwardly and shed many tears, often putting her hand to her mouth and shaking her head. By putting her hands to her mouth and lifting up her head, she tried to let us know she needed sustenance, having a starving desire for food and drink.

Toward night, we observed that the child was missing a tooth <note>original: "wanted a tooth"</note>, so that with the help of a quill, she sucked up some milk and received her drink in the same way. But to return to this child who was carried to bed in her fit: during the time of her prayers, as she inwardly mourned in her mind and was tormented, she would not stop until she had finished them. For though her increasing torments might interrupt her prayers, as soon as she had a little advantage over her enemy, she went on with them until she had ended them.

The next morning, which was the first of September, she was taken up in the same condition as she lay down, her teeth still remaining clenched <note>original: "fast together"</note>, yet she could receive milk through a quill as before. After dinner, she had a little struggle with her fit, in which her teeth were forced one over the other, whereas before they were only pressed against one another; the Devil being so malicious that now she could not receive any nourishment by the quill. Seeing, therefore, that it was impossible to preserve her life without a supply of nourishment, and because she often said she would not be well until she came to Warboys <note>original: "Warboyse"</note>, or at least a mile on her way there, we resolved to try what this would do. Therefore, in the company of Mrs. Pickering, she was set on horseback; at which she immediately rejoiced, making signs with her hands for them to go forward.

As soon as she had traveled about a mile on the way—though it was not the direct road to Warboys <note>Warboys: The village in Huntingdonshire where the alleged witches lived and where the children’s fits often intensified.</note>, it happened to be the same way she had come to Tichmarsh <note>original: "Tichmerch"</note> Grove—she began to be more cheerful. Her teeth were unclenched <note>original: "untied"</note>, and she immediately spoke and said, "I have not yet gone a mile; I shall soon, and then I shall be well." Shortly after, she rubbed her eyes and came to her senses, yet she wondered how and why she had come there, as well as at the company and the strangers who were present. 

Afterward, getting down from the horse, her leg was restored to her—a leg she had not been able to use for three weeks before—and she asked her aunt to pray for God to bless her. Thus, she walked on perfectly, and food <note>original: "Meat"; a general term for solid food at the time.</note> being brought to her, she ate and drank cheerfully. Then she took a prayer book and read for a good while, but when she came to the word *Satan*, it shook and wrenched <note>original: "wrung"</note> her shoulders. 

The Devil was so malicious that as soon as mention was made of their going home, she was immediately seized with a shaking of her shoulders, arms, and body, as if it would have crumpled her up <note>original: "shuffled her together"</note>. A little after, she arose, and as soon as she turned her face toward home, her eyes were shut, her legs were taken from her, and her teeth were tightly clenched in her head; her belly began to heave and swell, just as when she was first seized with the fits. 

But as long as she was going toward Warboys, without any mention of going back, she was cheerful and well; but if you stood still and talked of going home, she immediately sank into your arms as if in a faint <note>original: "swoon"</note>, struggling between life and death. However, as soon as you turned her face the other way, she immediately recovered and was restored to her health. This was proved several times, both on foot and on horseback, so that at last we were obliged to bring her back the same way she came; she was like a dead child to look upon in terms of her senses or motion, but had a very lovely and beautiful complexion,

That condition remained not at all altered. At night, by the grace of God, she received milk with the help of a quill <note>original: "Quill"; likely a hollow feather used as a straw or feeding tube.</note>, as before. Yet this was not without some difficulty, as her mouth <note>original: "the Place"</note> was more tightly shut than it had been at first. Thus, while indoors, she was fed milk like a nursing infant <note>original: "Suckling"</note>; and if food <note>original: "Meat"; a general term for solid food.</note> was brought to her, she pointed toward the place where she was accustomed to waking up.

The next day, therefore, after dinner, she was carried out again. When she arrived at the same spot, she began to rub her eyes and awoke. Gasping once or twice, she stretched out her arms and ate her food with a good appetite, as before. But when she was about to give thanks <note>original: "return Thanks"; to say grace or a prayer of gratitude.</note>, the Devil appeared again in his own likeness and tried to prevent that good deed by stopping her from speaking. He also twisted and contorted her body so that she could not speak a single proper word; indeed, the more holy the word, the more difficult it was for her to express it.

After this, it was thought useful to put a small stick into her mouth to keep her teeth apart. This was attempted, so that when she was moved around, she held the stick fast in her mouth, which kept her teeth open. However, she herself remained in a deep sleep, with few signs of strength or life appearing in her. She continued in this state until she was turned around again—which was not done quickly this time, as the observers were willing to see what would happen. 

A little while later, she struggled with her hands to pull the stick out of her mouth, moaning inwardly as if it caused her great distress. Yet she held it so tightly with her teeth that it could not be removed without great force, as was evident by the dents her teeth had made in the wood by the time she recovered. We did not dare to try this a second time because she complained that her mouth felt very cold since the stick had kept it open. And now we told her about the quill she had used—

From what has been related of this, you may guess what might happen to the rest of the sisters, who were no less tormented than she was, and some in an even more grievous manner; but to relate the particulars of all their misfortunes would be too long and tedious.

About a month after Mr. *Pickering* had carried this child to his house, Lady *Cromwell*—wife of Sir *Henry Cromwell*, Knight <note>original: "Kt."</note> (who then resided <note>original: "lay"</note> in *Ramsey*, a town two miles from *Warboys*)—came to Master *Throckmorton's* house with her daughter-in-law, Mistress *Cromwell*, to visit the children and to comfort their parents; but before she had

Having been there for a long time, the children all fell into their fits and were so terribly tormented that it moved the good lady's heart with pity to see them. She could not hold back her tears and ordered old *Mother Samuel* <note>Mother Samuel: Alice Samuel, an elderly neighbor who was the primary person accused in the Warboys witch trials.</note> to be sent for. She dared not refuse to come because her husband was a tenant of *Sir Henry Cromwell* <note>Sir Henry Cromwell: A wealthy local landowner and the grandfather of the future Oliver Cromwell.</note>. However, after she arrived, the children grew even worse than they were before.

Then Lady *Cromwell* took *Mother Samuel* aside and strictly charged her with this witchcraft, using threatening words. But she stubbornly denied everything and said that *Mr. Throckmorton* and his wife did her a great deal of wrong by blaming her without cause. To this, the lady answered that neither *Mr. Throckmorton* nor his wife accused her, but rather the children themselves in their fits—or more accurately, the spirit within them.

*Mistress Joan* <note>original: "Mrs. Joan"; at this time, "Mistress" was a title of respect for a young girl of the gentry, not necessarily a married woman.</note>, who was then in her fit, heard the old woman defending herself. Although she could not hear the lady or anyone else, she said that it was this woman who caused all of this and that something had told her so just then. She asked if nobody heard it but herself, claiming that it squeaked so loudly in her ear that she wondered they could not hear it, and she asked the old woman to listen to see if she could hear it; but *Mother Samuel* still continued to deny it.

Then Lady *Cromwell* wanted to take her up into a room to examine her more strictly, with *Dr. Hall*, a clergyman <note>original: "Divine"; a member of the clergy or a theologian.</note>, being present. But the old woman would by no means go with them and made several excuses to go home. When the lady found that neither she nor anyone else could persuade her, and seeing that she wanted to leave, she suddenly pulled off the woman's kerchief <note>original: "Kircher"; a cloth used to cover the head.</note> and, with a pair of scissors, cut off a lock of her hair. She gave it privately to *Mrs. Throckmorton* along with the woman's hair ribbon <note>original: "Hairlace"; a cord or ribbon used to tie the hair.</note>, asking her to burn them.

*Mother Samuel*, finding herself treated this way, spoke to the lady: "Madam, why do you treat me like this? I never did you any harm as yet." These

Words were afterwards remembered, though they were not noticed at the time. Towards night the Lady departed, leaving the children much as she had found them.

That night, after Lady *Cromwell* left *Warboys*, she was greatly troubled in a dream about *Mother Samuel*. As she imagined it, she was severely disturbed in her sleep by a cat which *Mother Samuel* had sent to her, which attempted to rip the skin and flesh off her bones and arms. The struggle between the cat and the Lady was so violent in her bed that night, and she made such a terrible noise, that she woke her bedfellow *Mistress Cromwell*, wife to the honorable *Mr. Oliver Cromwell* <note>Oliver Cromwell: This refers to Sir Oliver Cromwell (c. 1562–1655), the uncle and godfather of the future Lord Protector, Oliver Cromwell.</note>, son and heir to *Sir Henry Cromwell*, who was away from home that night.

*Mistress Cromwell*, seeing the Lady so distressed, woke her. The Lady thanked her for doing so and told her how much she had been troubled by *Mother Samuel* and her *cat*, along with many other details. These thoughts made her so uneasy that she could not rest for the remainder of the night for fear of the same dream returning. Soon after, the Lady fell very sick and remained so until her dying day, which was four years and three months after her visit to *Warboys*. The nature of her fits was much like those of the children, except that she remained fully conscious throughout them. Sometimes the pain would be in one arm, and sometimes in another; sometimes in one leg, and then it would move into the other, and it was often in her head. Sometimes it would seize only one or two fingers and would always shake the part affected, as if she had the palsy <term>Palsie: A historical term for paralysis or tremors, often used to describe conditions like Parkinson's disease or the after-effects of a stroke.</term>. And that saying of *Mother Samuel's* at *Warboys*—"Madam, I never hurt you yet"—would never leave her mind. Leaving this good lady now in heaven with God, we shall return to the children.

Around *Christmas* after 1590, *Mr. Henry Pickering* <note>Henry Pickering: A scholar and relative of the family who would later become a primary source for the written accounts of the Warboys trials.</note>, being then a student at *Cambridge*, went to *Mr. Throckmorton's house* and stayed there three or four

After a few days, being eager to speak with Mother *Samuel* and choosing a suitable time, Mr. Pickering asked two or three other scholars of his acquaintance to go with him. They agreed, and they went without the knowledge of anyone belonging to Master *Throckmorton’s* house. As they were going, she came out of her own house and crossed the street in front of them; they chose to follow her wherever she went rather than wait for her return, because her husband was an ill-tempered <note>original: "cross"</note> man who would not allow her to talk with anyone if he knew about it. 

She went to a neighbor’s house for yeast <note>original: "barm or yest"</note>, where the scholars followed her immediately and began to ask her questions. However, she was very impatient and reluctant to stay, refusing to let anyone speak but herself. They asked her to be quieter, but she answered that she was "born in a mill and conceived in a kiln," and must have her way and could speak no softer. <note>"born in a mill, begot in a kiln": A proverbial expression suggesting someone is naturally loud, stubborn, or used to noisy environments.</note> 

The majority of her talk was a tirade against Mr. *Throckmorton* and his children. She claimed he mistreated her by allowing his children to accuse her and damage her reputation. She argued that their illness <note>original: "distemper"</note> came from their own naughtiness <note>original: "wantonness"</note> and that if they were her children, she would punish them for it. 

Then they questioned her about her service to God and her faith, to which she replied that her God would deliver her, defend her, and take revenge on her enemies. Then one of them asked her if she served the same God that others did, to which she answered, "Yes." However, they had a great deal of trouble getting her to stop using the phrase "her God" and instead name the "God of Heaven and Earth." 

At last, she told them that if she stayed any longer, her husband would beat her. Then Mr. *Pickering* told her that God's vengeance would surely wait for her, regardless of how she might deceive the world and herself, and that the only way to prevent God’s vengeance was through confession and repentance.

She suddenly said, "Now my uncle and two others" (whom she named) "are going to Mother Samuel; we shall hear news shortly."

When Mr. Throckmorton heard this, and heard his daughter further say, "Now Mr. Pickering and Mother Samuel have parted," he asked about Mr. Pickering. Finding he had gone out, Mr. Throckmorton supposed he was at Mother Samuel's house. Therefore, going out of doors to look for him, he met him in the churchyard and told him what had happened. 

Upon hearing this, Mr. Pickering came into the parlor where the children were and spoke with them. They were able to hear no one but him, and he found that they could exactly repeat every word and event that had passed between him and Mother Samuel. They noted, however, that there was so much wind that they had great difficulty <note>original: "much adoe"</note> hearing what was said, even though at that same time there was no wind at all.

After this, the spirit would appear to them several times in the form of a dun chicken <note>A "dun chicken" refers to a grayish-brown bird; in this context, it is a "familiar," a spirit supposedly serving a witch.</note>. It would talk familiarly with them, saying it came from Mother Samuel (whom it called "Dame") and was sent by her to the children to torment and vex them.

The Spirit would say what she was then doing at home, in what part of the house she was, or would tell exactly where she was located. This was proven true by a messenger sent specifically to discover the truth.

And now the Spirit began to accuse Mother Samuel to the children during their fits, telling them it was she who had bewitched them and all the servants in the house. It also claimed that whenever they were in their fits and were carried to Mother Samuel's house, or if she was made to come to them, they would be cured. This was often proven and never once failed. For if the children were carried to Mother Samuel's house while in their fits, as soon as they came to the door, they would rub their eyes and say they were well, asking, "*Why are you carrying me? Set me down,*" as if they were ashamed to be carried through the streets, not knowing what condition they had been in. As long as they stayed in the house, they were well; but once they thought of going away or attempted to go out the doors, they fell down on the ground and were brought away in the same condition they had been carried there.

Furthermore, when Mother Samuel went to Mr. Throckmorton's house, even if the children were in the greatest extremity <note>extreme physical or mental distress</note>, as soon as she entered the parlor or hall where they were, the children would immediately jump to their feet and be as well as anyone else in the house. They would continue to be well as long as she stayed, but when she was about to leave, they would fall to the ground like stones. If she turned around and came back toward them, they would be well again—a test that was tried twenty times in a single hour. When she finally went away, she left them in the same condition she had found them.
