After this, Mr. Throckmorton decided to separate his children and send them away to stay with his friends to see how they would be treated there; however, he always kept some of them at home with him.

It would be too tiresome to recount everything that happened to them while they were away, but this was remarkable: even though they were eight, ten, or eleven miles apart, they could tell what was happening to each other during their fits. They would say, "*Now my sister is being harshly treated*," <note>original: "sore handled"</note> just as she herself was experiencing a fit at that very moment. This was proven to be true by comparing the timing and other circumstances.

While they were away, they were never all completely free from their fits at the same time, though some of them did not have a fit for a month or even half a year, and one of them was free of them for an entire year. Others, however, were rarely without them for more than three days, except since the last *Lent Assizes* <note>Lent Assizes: Periodic regional courts held during Lent for civil and criminal trials.</note>, when those witches were executed.

Skipping over what happened for nearly a year and a half, we shall proceed to more recent times. At that point—whether the spirits were moved by their own malice, by those who sent them, or by the parents' impatience—it pleased God to allow them the liberty to exercise their malice against these children. Consequently, in these later times, they were tormented in body and mind even more than before. At that time, four of them were at their father’s house in *Warboys* <note>original: "Warboyse"</note>, and the other, who was the eldest, was at Mr. *Pickering’s* at *Tichmarsh Grove* <note>original: "Tichmersh-Grove"</note>.

Around this time, which was in the year 1592, the second-youngest daughter, being about fourteen years of age, fell into a very strange fit. Every day for about three weeks, she had a senseless fit at one time of the day or another, and sometimes many times in a single day. But during this fit she could

...she could neither hear, see, nor speak to anyone. Besides her internal grief, she would heave, startle, and her body would swell up, which was very distressing for her at the time. She would do any task she understood needed to be done through signs and would pay her respects as she passed by to those to whom it was due. Consequently, those who were unaware of her condition could not perceive that she was unwell; yet she could neither speak to anyone, nor hear anyone speak to her, except for the times she would chatter to a newborn infant, which she took a great deal of delight in.

In the beginning of these fits, an aunt of hers gave birth to a child in the house, and several of their friends stayed there for a week or ten days. During this entire time, this child was in these fits. When Mother Samuel <note>original: "Mother Samuel" — Alice Samuel, an elderly neighbor accused by the family of being the witch responsible for the children's fits.</note> came to the house like the rest of the guests, she was brought up to the lady’s bedroom <note>original: "Gentlewoman's Chamber"</note>, where most of the company usually gathered. She brought Mother Samuel both meat and drink and would do anything the old woman desired.

At this, the child wondered and said she was glad her aunt had given birth. She then asked why several of her aunts and uncles said they would be there, when they were actually already in the house, and some of them were even in the room at that moment. Mother Samuel told her that the people she was asking for were present; but the girl said she saw nobody but her and the little child in her arms, even though she looked directly into their faces. Continuing in this condition for three weeks, she finally came out of her aunt’s chamber into the hall, where, in a short time, she fell into a very troublesome fit; however, it did not last long. Her mother being in the hall, the girl asked for her blessing <note>It was a standard Early Modern custom for children to kneel and ask for their parents' formal blessing daily.</note>; and inquiring about several things, she asked how her aunt was, from whom she had just come. Nor could she remember anything that had happened over the past three weeks.

But both her troubles and those of the rest of her sisters grew more severe as the year drew to a close, their fits every day growing more painful and behaving in a strange manner.

Around Allhallowtide <note>original: "Hollantide" — The season of All Saints' Day (November 1st), traditionally a time associated with supernatural occurrences.</note>, the spirits became very familiar with the children. When the fits were almost at an end, the spirits would talk with them for half an hour or more about the nature of the fits they were going to have. They also spoke concerning Mother Samuel, claiming it was her pleasure that they should be treated in that manner; but the children said several times that they would bring her to shame for it at last. The spirits also told them how long their fits would last, when they would have the next one, and what the nature of them would be—whether they would be more or less painful. This information was set down in writing exactly as the children spoke it, and it always proved to be perfectly true.

The times and signs which the spirits appointed for the beginning or ending of their fits were specific: they would happen in the morning as soon as the children attempted to get out of bed, or as soon as they were up and dressed; as soon as they asked their father, mother, or grandmother for a blessing; as soon as they took a book in their hand to pray, or when they had finished their prayers; and as soon as they went to breakfast, or when dinner was set upon the table;

...or as soon as they sat down to dinner, or when the food <note>original: "meat"</note> was first put into their mouths; when dinner was ended, or when they had put away their knives after dinner—the same patterns were observed at supper. Or if it were on the Sabbath day <note>Sunday</note>, or when their bellies were to be twisted <note>This likely refers to hunger pains or stomach cramps associated with the timing of meals or the ringing of church bells</note>, as soon as the first, second, or third peal <note>of church bells</note> was rung or finished, along with many other such signs that marked the beginning or ending of their fits.

When they had continued in this condition for over a month—whether the Devil grew weary of it (for he often told the children during their fits that he was tired of his mistress <note>original: "Dame"</note>, Mother *Samuel*) or whether, through God’s providence, the spirits found they could not kill the children as they desired—they told the children that in a short time they would bring their mistress to either a confession or to ruin <note>original: "confusion"</note>. Now they began to accuse Mother *Samuel* openly to her face, telling her that they would not be well in any place except her house, or unless she was forced to stay with them. If one of these things did not happen, they said their fits would be more violent than ever.

Mr. *Throckmorton*, still thinking the spirits might be lying, was resolved to test them to the limit for three weeks. During that entire time, the children suffered very severe fits, so that by nightfall, not one of them was able to go to bed alone. Their legs were full of pain and sores, in addition to other unusual ailments. For that entire period, one of the girls never had the use of her legs, except for an hour or two a day while Mother *Samuel* was in the house. Otherwise, her legs were pulled up against her body as if they had been tied with strings, so that wherever you sat her down, she was forced to stay unless she crawled away.

Soon after she had gone, some of the children fell into their fits <note>seizures or hysterical episodes attributed to demonic possession</note> again. Then the spirit talked with them and told them that Mother *Samuel* was then feeding her spirits and making a new pact <note>original: "league"</note> with them. The agreement was that even if she came back to the house, the children would not be any better, but rather worse for her being there, because she was not willing to stay there any longer. 

This accordingly proved true; for when she returned, those who were in their fits continued in them, and those who were not fell into them after her arrival. They cried out, "Now Mother *Samuel* has made a new agreement <note>original: "composition"</note> with her spirits, and now they shall be no better, but worse!"

Mr. *Throckmorton*, coming home and perceiving the situation, could not help but be concerned. Yet, leaving everything to God Almighty, he would not allow the old woman to leave his house, since his children, when in their fits, could neither hear, see, nor speak to anyone but her; and some of them could consume nothing except what she gave them or touched with her hands.

As Mother *Samuel* remained in the house, she could do or say nothing in any part of it without the children revealing it while in their fits—especially when she was feeding her spirits. For then the children would say, "Now Mother *Samuel* is in such a place feeding her spirits." When the observers went to look, they found her there, but they could not discover exactly what she was doing.

Mr. Throckmorton, desiring to make an experiment of this matter, asked Mother Samuel one night to predict how many fits the children would have the next day, what kind of fits they would be, when they would begin, and how long they would last. They then examined her chin but could see no marks of any kind; it was clean and smooth except for some small red spots resembling flea bites. Mr. *Throckmorton* asked her if her chin often bled like that, to which she answered, "Very often." He then asked her who could witness this, and she said nobody, for it always bled when she was alone, and she had never told anyone about it.

After she was condemned <note>found guilty and sentenced to death</note>, she confessed that when Mr. *Throckmorton* had questioned her, the spirits were actually sucking from her <note>A common belief in witchcraft trials was that spirits or "familiars" sucked blood from a "witch's mark" or "teat" on the accused person's body</note>. But to set such matters aside and move on to the old woman's confession: by this time, Mother *Samuel* began to grow weary of staying at Mr. *Throckmorton*’s house. This was not only because she could do nothing in the house without the children revealing it during their fits, but especially because the children told her plainly to her face that she must confess these things before the *Tuesday* after *Twelfth-day* <note>January 6, the feast of the Epiphany, marking the end of the Christmas season</note>. They said the spirits had told them that they would force her to confess whether she wanted to or not.

At that time, no one expected that this *Tuesday* would be the day of the court sessions at *Huntingdon* <note>original: "Huntington"</note>. Yet the children were often whispering to themselves about that *Tuesday*, which they longed for intensely. The spirits had told them that after that day, they would have no more fits. They further added that if she confessed before that *Tuesday*, they would recover even sooner.

The children often begged her to confess so that they might be healthy again, but she continued to deny the charges. She asked why she should confess to something that was not true—something she knew nothing about and had never consented to. The children answered that they did not want her to accuse herself falsely and told her to be careful of that; yet, during their fits, they gave her very good and holy <note>original: "divine"</note> advice regarding her confession on several occasions.

The speeches they made to her at various times were to this effect: they described the joys of Heaven she would lose and the torments of Hell she would endure if she were guilty and did not confess. Conversely, they told her what advantages she might gain if she confessed and was sorry for what she had done.

...gnawing at her heart. The next day, the pain would be in her knee or lower, so that she went limping <note>original: "halting"</note> up and down the house. Mrs. *Throckmorton* got out of her bed and felt her belly; she found a swelling there as Mother Samuel had complained, but did not feel it move. She did not stay long, for the weather was very cold. 

Undoubtedly, she was then pregnant with <note>original: "breeding"</note> the child she later used to "plead her belly" <note>To "plead the belly" was a legal plea used by women under a death sentence; if they could prove they were pregnant, their execution was stayed at least until the child was born.</note> after her sentence of condemnation. But she still complained of her belly and said to Mr. *Throckmorton* that she had often been told some evil spirit haunted his house and tormented his children. This, he told her, he did believe. 

Then she said, "*I believe one of them has gotten into my belly.*" 

"*That,*" says Mr. *Throckmorton*, "*may be true.*" 

So she said it was an evil house, haunted with evil spirits, and wished she had never entered it. He told her that if any evil spirits haunted the house, they were of her sending, and so he agreed with everything she said. In the morning, she complained much but said the swelling in her belly was gone. She could not say where the greatest pain was because it was in so many places, though her appetite <note>original: "stomach"</note> was the best part of her.

But the more she prayed, the worse it became; and when she mentioned God or *Jesus Christ*, the fit grew more violent. But that fit was so terrifying to Mother *Samuel* that she prayed afterward that she might never see the like again.

The children all kept urging Mother *Samuel* to confess, saying she must do it in the end, and that if she would allow them to be well before Christmas <note>original: "Christmass"</note>, they would feel grateful to her. As soon as she had finished, the three children—who were then in their fits and had been in that condition for three weeks—wiped their eyes, quickly pushed back the stools they were sitting on, and stood up on their feet, being as well as they had ever been in their lives.

Mr. Throckmorton’s face was then toward the children and his back to the old woman; seeing them start up all at once, he said, *"Thanks be to God."* In the meantime, the old woman fell down on her knees behind him and said, *"Good master, forgive me."* Turning around and seeing her down, he said, *"Why, Mother Samuel, what is the matter?" "Oh sir,"* she said, *"I have been the cause of all this trouble to your children." "Have you, Mother Samuel?"*

...he said: *“And why? What cause did I ever give you to treat me and my children like this?”* “None at all,” she said. Then he said, *“You have done me a greater wrong then.”* “Good Master,” she said, “forgive me.” *“God forgive you,”* he said, *“and I do. But tell me, how did you become such a woman?”* “Master,” she said, *“I have forsaken my Maker and given my soul to the Devil.”*

Then the grandmother and mother of the children, who were in the hall and heard them speaking so loudly in the parlor, came in. Mother Samuel asked for their pardon as well. Mrs. Throckmorton, the mother, immediately forgave her with all her heart, though she did not quite understand what was the matter. Then Mother Samuel asked for forgiveness from the three children who were present and the rest, and she kissed them; the children forgave her easily.

Mr. Throckmorton and his wife, seeing the old woman so penitent and dejected—as she was weeping and lamenting the entire time—did all they could to comfort her. They told her they would freely forgive her from their hearts, provided their children were no longer troubled. She said she trusted in God that they would never be troubled again; yet she could not be comforted. Mr. Throckmorton then sent for Dr. Dorrington, the town's minister, and told him all the circumstances. Everyone tried to put her at ease, but she nevertheless wept all that night.

The next day, being Christmas Eve and the Sabbath <note>Sabbath: In this context, Sunday.</note>, Dr. Dorrington chose a text about repentance from the Psalms. Sharing her confession with the assembly, he focused his sermon primarily on that subject to comfort a penitent heart, hoping it might affect her. Throughout the sermon, Mother Samuel wept and lamented, and she was frequently so loud in her emotional outbursts <note>original: "Passions"</note> that she drew the eyes of the entire congregation upon her.

But Mr. *Throckmorton*, reflecting on Mother *Samuel*’s previous unreliability <note>original: "Inconstancy"</note>, and noting that there were no witnesses to her confession except himself, the doctor, and those of his own household—lest they should be thought biased—requested that after prayers, she should come into the center <note>original: "Body"</note> of the church. There, he demanded that she declare whether the confession she made before him and the doctor was forced from her, or whether she made it freely and of her own accord. She answered before them all that it came from her freely, and she requested that her neighbors would pray to God for her and forgive her.

Towards evening, Mr. *Dorrington*, understanding she still continued in this sorrowful condition, came to Mr. *Throckmorton*’s house and requested him, out of pity, to let her go home to her husband, promising that he would try to reconcile them. The old woman was very inclined to do this, and Mr. *Throckmorton* readily consented and tried to encourage the plan, little suspecting that any ill consequence might happen because of it. Her husband, hearing of it, spoke bluntly as usual and said she could come home if she wished.

But, without doubt, she received a cold welcome from both her husband and her daughter for confessing this matter. We heard from her later that both of them turned against her and persuaded her to deny everything she had said, claiming it was not so. The next day, being *Christmas Day*, she absolutely denied everything she had said. Word soon reached Mr. *Throckmorton*’s ears that this new convert had recanted <note>original: "revolted"</note>. Although he had been well satisfied with her open confession in the church, he could scarcely believe what was being reported of her.

However, the neighbors told her it was too late then to deny it, since they had all heard it, and they told her the exact place where they had been standing.

At this, she pretended to fall into a faint <note>original: "Swoon"</note>, but she recovered again quickly. That same day, Mr. *Throckmorton* resolved to continue his journey and clear his name in the matter; he ordered the old woman and her daughter to be taken to the Bishop of *Lincoln* to be examined.

### *The* Examination *of* Alice Samuel, *of* Warboys *in the County of* Huntingdon, *taken at* Buckden, *before the Right Reverend Father in God,* William, *by God’s Permission, Bishop of* Lincoln, *on the 26th of December, 1592.*

When asked whether a dun <note>original: "Dun"; a dull grayish-brown color</note> chicken did ever suck on her chin, and how often, the accused <note>original: "Examinant"</note> said that it sucked twice, and no more, since last Christmas Eve. When asked whether it was a natural chicken—because when it came to her chin she could scarcely feel it, yet when she wiped it off, her chin bled—she further said that the dun chicken first came to her chin and sucked before it ever went to Mr. *Throckmorton’s* house. She also stated that the evil and trouble <note>A reference to the fits and symptoms of demonic possession</note> that afflicted Mr. *Throckmorton’s* children happened because of the dun chicken, which she knows has now left both the children and her. Furthermore, she said that Mr. *Throckmorton* and Dr. *Dorrington* would bring further information regarding things that she has not yet declared.

## *The Examination of Alice Samuel of Warboys, taken at Buckden on the 29th Day of December 1592, before the Reverend Father in God, William, by God’s Permission, Bishop of Lincoln; Francis Cromwell and Richard Tryce, Esquires, Justices of Her Majesty’s Peace, for the County aforesaid.*

SHE says she never did harm to anyone, except for the children in question. When asked how she knows the aforementioned Dun Chicken <note>original: "Dun Chicken"; a spirit or familiar in the form of a grayish-brown bird</note> has left the said children, she says it is because the Dun Chicken, along with the others, has now entered her. They are now in the bottom of her belly and make her feel so full that she feels as if she might burst; she says that this morning they caused her to be so bloated that she could hardly lace her coat. She added that on the way here, they weighed so heavy that the horse she rode <note>original: "rid"</note> was not able to carry her. 

Furthermore, she confessed that the "upright man" <note>In the context of 16th-century witchcraft, this likely refers to a human-shaped manifestation of the Devil or a master spirit</note> she told *Mr. Throckmorton* about had told her that Throckmorton was a harsh man and would cause her much trouble; therefore, he said he would give her "free spirits" <note>spirits or familiars at her disposal</note> that would harass and torment his children, and so he did. She confessed that these spirits often sucked her blood as a reward for what they did, always doing so before she sent them anywhere <note>original: "any whither"</note>. She also confessed that whatever the children said about the spirits was true, and that the spirits were present when the children saw them, and that she saw them as well. She admitted that she often gave a private signal <note>original: "Beck"; a nod or gesture of command</note> with her finger or head,

She and her daughter were committed to Huntingdon Gaol <note>original: "Goal"; a common archaic spelling of jail</note>, where she was suspected of causing the death of one of the jailer’s servants whom she had threatened. After dinner, Mr. *Throckmorton* requested that the High Sheriff and Justices accept bail for *Agnes Samuel*, so that he might take her home to his house to see whether the same evidence of guilt might appear in her as it had in her mother. This request was granted with much difficulty.

### The Report of Dr. *Dorrington* concerning what happened at *Warboys* on Tuesday, the Sessions Day at *Huntingdon*, the ninth of January.

About twelve o'clock, *Mary*, *Jane*, and *Grace*, daughters of Mr. *Throckmorton* of *Warboys*, Esq., fell into their usual fits of lameness, blindness, deafness, and lack of sensation; only their youngest brother *Robert*, who was nineteen years old, could speak to *Jane*, and she could speak to *Mary* and *Grace*. After dinner, Dr. *Dorrington* came to see them with a *Cambridge* scholar and found them all in their fits, with each of them often repeating the following words: *I am glad, I am glad, none so glad as I.* The doctor asked their brother to ask them why they were so glad. *Jane* answered, *Within two hours we shall have good news*, and her other sister confirmed the same. *Mary* and *Jane* whispered to each other, *I wonder how she should know that thing; I am sure no one in this house told her, and therefore her spirits must be telling her.* *Robert* was again requested to ask *Jane*, and *Jane* to ask *Mary* and *Grace*, when they

Everything happened exactly as they predicted. They immediately rose from their stools and went from the great parlor into an inner parlor to see how their mother was doing. After a short time, they rubbed their eyes two or three times and woke up, feeling perfectly well again. Shortly after *Agnes Samuel* was brought to Mr. *Throckmorton’s* house, Mistress *Jane*, the eldest daughter, was brought home from *Titchmarsh Grove* to *Warboys*. Leaving aside what she suffered while she remained there, we will specifically observe what happened at *Warboys*.

On *Friday*, February 9, 1592, Mistress *Jane* fell into her usual fits, complaining of great pain in her legs. They had been sore for nine or ten weeks prior, which was most of the time she was at *Titchmarsh Grove*. But now the pain in her legs grew much worse; for the previous two weeks <note>original: "fortnight"</note>, they were so painful that she could neither walk nor sit, but could only lie on her bed or on cushions by the fire. Her fit continued all day. At night, the spirit came to her, and she talked with it as she usually did, asking where it came from and what news it brought with a great deal of disdain. The "thing" would not tell her where it came from, but said she would have very severe fits in the future, worse than ever. During this time, she remained in full possession of her memory and senses. She answered that she neither cared for nor feared him, for God was on her side and would protect her. At this, the spirit departed, though she remained in her fit for most of the night until she went to bed.

On the afternoon of the tenth, she lay groaning in her fit by the fireside and was suddenly struck with a nosebleed. This surprised her very much, and she feared it was a sign of bad news to follow. When she—

"What?" she asked. "Do you say I shall be treated worse than I ever was? Ha! What are you saying? That I shall now have my fits while I can still hear, see, and recognize everyone? After this, she was silent for a while, but as if listening to something being said, she soon called for *Agnes Samuel*. She asked where Agnes was and said that she had too much liberty and needed to be more strictly watched; for lately, she had been in the kitchen chamber talking with her spirits, and had begged Mr. *Blue* not to let Jane have such extreme fits while she could still speak, hear, and recognize everyone. 

"But he says he will torment me more," Jane continued, "and not rest until Mistress <note>original: "Dame"</note> *Agnes Samuel* is brought to her end." The girl [Agnes] confessed she was in the kitchen chamber alone, but denied that she talked with spirits or knew of any such things. ...and what she had said, she answered that she had been asleep and knew of nothing she had said. She wondered how her handkerchief became so bloody, saying someone else must have bloodied it and not her, for she was not prone to nosebleeds.

At night, when her father and mother rose from supper, she fell into the fit Mr. Blue <note>original: "Mr. Blew"</note> had threatened her with, being severely convulsed <note>original: "twisted"</note> in every part of her body. Sometimes she would thrust out her arms so strongly and stiffly that it was impossible to bend them. Sometimes she would twist them backward so far that no one could do the same by their natural strength, crying out very piteously. Sometimes she complained of her stomach, saying she was very sick and felt as if she would vomit; at other times she complained of her head or her belly, with no part of her body being free from extreme pain. She often called upon God to remember her and to deliver her. Sometimes the fit would stop her breath for so long that when she finally breathed again, it was with a deep and loud groan. When she was often asked how she was doing, she answered that she was very sick and full of pain, saying that she heard and saw everyone present.

She continued in this condition for about half an hour, and while she was complaining, she fell into her most severe fit, having her mouth clamped shut and being deprived of all her senses. After remaining quiet for a while, she let out a great groan, and then, her mouth opening, she said, "This is quite a situation <note>original: "Here is a Rule indeed"; an expression meaning "this is a fine state of affairs" or referring to the spirit's conduct.</note>; I see you are as good as your word with me. Where did you come from, or what news do you bring, I ask you?" 

The thing answered that she would be treated even worse than this. She replied, "God is above the Devil, and do what you can, you shall not be able to hurt me. But why do you punish me more than all my sisters, giving me fits during which I recognize everyone?" 

They said it was because she told tales about their mistress. "Who is your mistress?" she asked. "Nan Samuel," <note>Nan is a common diminutive for Agnes; Nan Samuel was the daughter of Alice Samuel, the woman accused of bewitching the Throckmorton children in the famous Warboys witch trials.</note> it replied.

"I would rather you stay away until you are sent for; but what news have you brought?"

He said, "I told you I had been fighting last Sunday night, but I have had many battles since."

"So it seems," she said, "for *Pluck*, *Catch*, and *Blue* were all here, and they all came to me limping."

"Yes," he said, "I have encountered them all."

"But I wonder," she said, "how you could beat them, for they are very large and you are but a little one."

He said, "I am good enough for two of the best of them together."

"What’s that?" he asked.

"They will all fall upon you at once and beat you."

He said he did not care about that; he would beat two of the best of them.

"And who shall beat the other two?" she asked. "For there is one who has often been spoken of, called *Hardname*; his name consists of eight letters, and every letter stands for a word, but what his name is otherwise, we do not know."

The Spirit answered that his cousin *Smack* <note>original: "Smacks"</note> would help him beat the other two. (There are also two other *Smacks*, as appears from the old woman’s confession <note>This refers to the confession of Alice Samuel, the elderly woman accused of bewitching the Throckmorton children in the famous Warboys witch trial.</note>).

"What?" she said. "Will your cousin *Smack* help you? Is there kinship among devils? I never heard of that before; God keep me from such relatives!"

A great deal of such foolish talk passed between them, but at last the Spirit said, "You shall have no more fits like the ones you have had."

"No?" she said. "That’s well, but you can do nothing but lie."

"Why," he said, "will you not believe me?"

"No," she said, "should I believe the Father of Lies? <note>A traditional title for the Devil, referencing the biblical Book of John 8:44.</note>"

"But you shall find it to be true," he said.

She replied, "If I do, I will believe you, but not before. I pray to God it may be true, but whether it is or not, I don't care a straw <note>original: "care not a rush"; a "rush" is a marsh plant of little value, used as a common idiom for something worthless.</note> for you."

"No?" he said. "Will you not thank me?"

"Thank you? Hang you and all your companions! For I will believe you no further than I can see you, nor do I care for any of you at all."

This *Smack* has often endeavored to deceive her with fair promises—that he would do anything for her if she would only love him—but by the assistance of God, she always resisted and defied his temptations.

While she sat there in her fit, talking with the spirit Smack, one of her sisters sat by her—also in a quiet fit—who suddenly said to her, "Sister *Jane*, the spirit <note>original: "the Thing"; the children often referred to the supernatural entities or "familiars" as "the Thing."</note> tells me you will have a very severe <note>original: "sore"</note> fit soon." Immediately afterward, she fell into a very painful and distressing fit, with her body swelling and heaving as she groaned and cried out in her usual way. 

Then her father, Mr. *Throckmorton*, called for *Agnes Samuel* and asked her to hold the child, saying, "I think you are the most suitable <note>original: "fittest"</note> person to deal with the trouble of it." After the child had been in her arms for a considerable time, struggling and acting very distressed, her sister (the one who had predicted the painful fit) said, "Take comfort, Sister *Jane*, for the spirit tells me the worst is over and you will be well soon. The spirit says that *Agnes Samuel* is tired of holding you, and therefore you shall be well." This quickly proved true, for her distressing fit ended, and she remained quiet as she had been before. 

At this, her father, Mr. *Throckmorton*, told his cousin what his daughter *Jane* had said earlier: namely <note>original: "viz." (videlicet)</note>, that whenever a stranger arrived, she would fall into a fit, and then if *Agnes Samuel* said certain words, she would recover, proving Agnes was a witch. His cousin urged him to test this. 

Consequently, the child’s father ordered *Agnes Samuel* to repeat these words after him: "Just as I am a witch and consented to the death of Lady *Cromwell*, so I command <note>original: "charge"</note> the spirit to depart and let her be well." The woman <note>original: "Maid"</note> began to speak, but she either could not or would not say them clearly. She kept saying, "Consenting to our death of our Lady *Cromwell*," and even after repeating them three or four times, she would not speak the words clearly. Because of this, the girl who was in her fit said, "Something here is telling me that she will not say them correctly." Finally, with much difficulty, she spoke the words, and immediately the girl came out of her fit and was perfectly well. These same words were used for another sister during a fit, and they were similarly successful.

The next day at dinner, Mrs. <note>original: "Mrs."; in this period, "Mistress" or "Mrs." was a title of respect used for young, unmarried girls of the gentry, similar to the modern "Miss."</note> *Elizabeth* being in the middle of her fit, was assigned to say grace <note>original: "say Grace"; a short prayer of thanks before a meal.</note>. When she had gone through half of it, her mouth was suddenly shut tight so that she could not finish it. Then Mr. *Throckmorton* called for *Agnes Samuel* and asked her to command the spirit to open the girl's mouth again so that she might finish the grace. Agnes did so, and the child continued to the end of it. Then Mr. *Throckmorton* tried to teach *Agnes Samuel* a short grace herself, but although the children recited it to her two or three times a day, she was unable to learn it.

On the following Thursday, Mrs. *Mary*, who had been well ever since the court sessions <note>original: "Sessions Day"; the day of the legal proceedings at Huntingdon.</note> at *Huntingdon*, felt somewhat uneasy in the morning. Around nine o'clock, she fell into a great trembling and shaking, and her whole body shook uncontrollably <note>original: "could not hold a Joint of her."</note> without any known cause. Soon after, she fell into a very troublesome fit which lasted for half an hour. As she finally began to recover, she said, "Is it true? Do you say this is the day that I must scratch the young witch? I am glad of it; I will get even with her <note>original: "pay her home"; to deliver full punishment or revenge.</note> both for myself and my sisters." Mr. *Edward* and Mr. *Henry Pickering*, along with several others standing by, had the maid <note>In this context, "the maid" likely refers to Agnes Samuel’s daughter, who was also accused of witchcraft, though the text later names Agnes herself.</note> brought into the chamber where the child was to see what would happen, knowing that the maid was strong enough to prevent herself from being scratched even if three children as small as Mary attacked her. 

As soon as the maid entered the chamber where the child was, Mary cried out, "Are you here, you young witch, who has done all this mischief?" *Agnes Samuel* seemed to stand amazed at these words, not being used to hearing such expressions from the child. One person in the company then asked Agnes to take the child in her arms and carry her downstairs, for the use of her legs had been taken from her <note>A common symptom in these cases was a temporary paralysis or inability to walk during or after a fit.</note>.

Miss <note>original: "Mrs."; a title of respect used for young, unmarried girls of the gentry.</note> Mary let the maid <note>This refers to Agnes "Nan" Samuel, the daughter of the accused witch Alice Samuel. The children often called her "the young witch."</note> pick her up very quietly in her arms, and she clasped her hands around the maid's neck. But as she began to lift her up, the child started scratching her so violently and fiercely—to the amazement of those standing by—saying, "I will scratch you, you young witch, and get revenge on you <note>original: "pay you home"; to deliver full punishment or retribution.</note> for punishing me and my sisters! The Thing <note>The child is referring to a "familiar spirit" or demon that she believes is communicating with her.</note> tells me I would have had no more fits, but would have been well, if not for you." The maid stood still, holding down her head (for the child was kneeling on the maid's knees) and cried out piteously; yet she either would not or could not pull her head away even once. "No," said the child, "I know you are crying, but the spirit said I should not hear you, so that I would not pity you; and it is he who holds you so that you cannot get away from me."

The child scratched until the skin came off the width of a shilling, but there was no blood, only clear fluid <note>original: "Water." In witchcraft lore, wounds inflicted during fits were often described as not bleeding normally.</note>. At last, the maid brought her down into the parlor, where the child sat for a while on her stool. She seemed to be very sorry and said, "I would not have scratched Nan Samuel like that, but the Thing commanded me to do it and forced me to it—stretching out my arms and straining my fingers whether I wanted to or not—and made me scratch her." This indeed appeared to be contrary to the child's natural character. The child remained in her fit until an hour after dinner, and when she came out of it, she would not believe what she had done until she saw the maid's face; then she cried and was sorry for it.

The next day, being Friday, Miss Mary was in a very mild fit all morning. A little before dinner, she said to her sisters, who were also in their fits, "I am glad, very glad," but she would not say why. Presently she said to the Thing that stood by her, "But I know you will lie to me, as you have often done." "No," said the Thing, "I am not accustomed to lying." "Then don't," she said. "Who are you, pray?" The Thing answered, "*Smack*." <note>Smack was the name of one of the several spirits or "familiars" that the Throckmorton children claimed were haunting them.</note> "What?" she said—

### WITCHCRAFT, &c.

She asked, "Are you that *Smack* who usually comes to my sister *Jane* and tells her so many things?" 

"Yes," he said, adding that he had never told her sister *Jane* any lie yet, nor was this a lie that he was telling her now. Then Miss <note>original: "Mrs."; a title of respect used for young, unmarried girls of the gentry.</note> *Mary* told her sisters (all of them being in their fits) what *Smack* had told her: which was that after dinner she would come out of her fit and never have any more, because she had scratched *Agnes Samuel*. This happened just as he said.

The same day, being the second of February, Miss *Jane* fell into her fit while in bed. It seemed *Smack* had been with her and told her something, which he afterward declared to her sisters while they were in their fits. When she went down into the parlor to join them and had sat for a while by the fire, she spoke to something next to her: 

"I will not look at you, for you never come unless you bring bad news. I was sick the last time you were here, and I wonder who sent for you?" 

The thing answered, "You were sick the last time I was here, but now you shall be much worse." 

"Do your worst," she said, "for God will preserve me. I do not fear you. I wonder," she added, "how your *leg* is? I think *Smack* spoke with you when he broke your *leg*." <note>In the testimony of the Throckmorton children, the "familiars" or spirits were often described as fighting or injuring one another.</note>

The spirit said, "I beg you, do not tell *Smack* that I was here." 

"You beg me?" she said. "I do not know that *Smack*, but if I did, I would tell him so that he might break your neck too." 

The thing answered, "Yes, you know him well enough, for you made him break my *leg*. But I would not for anything have him know that I was here," said the Thing. "But I will make you pay for all of this coming week." 

"Will you?" she said. "I will bet <note>original: "lay with you"; to make a wager.</note> you whatever you want that I shall not be sick this week if I choose not to be, for I know one thing." 

He said, "I know very well that *Smack* was with you this morning; but despite what both of you can do, you shall have a sick week of it, and that..."

She asked, "Why do you take revenge on me instead of *Smack*?" 

He said he did not dare deal with him, for *Smack* was too strong for him.

After a little silence, she asked her sisters if they saw *Catch*, who had been talking with her just now; they said no. 

"Then perhaps he is gone," she said, "for I cannot see him." 

Then she began to talk softly with them, as if she didn't want anyone else to hear, saying that this morning *Smack* had been with her and told her she would have a very bad week next week, which he could not prevent. But he said he had beaten *Pluck* so badly that he would never come near her again; and if he did, *Smack* said he would kill him. He then told her, "If you will go to some friend’s house and stay all week, then you will be well." 

But she replied, "I told him I had no friend’s house to go to, and I wouldn't go anyway, no matter what he did." 

Then he asked her to go to *Somersham* <note>original: "Sommerson"; a town in Cambridgeshire.</note>, a town two miles from *Warboys*, where she had been very healthy while staying with an uncle of hers. 

"But," she said, "I wonder how he knows *Somersham*; I think he knows the whole countryside. But I told him I would not stir a foot for him, no matter what he could do." 

Then they asked when *Smack* would come again; she said he told her he would return after supper. In the meantime, some strangers arrived who were eager to see her while she was out of her fit. These fits were attributed to *Agnes Samuel*, as she was accused of being a witch and of consenting to the death of the *Lady Cromwell*.

At night, shortly before supper, she fell into her fit again and continued in it until supper was finished. When the company had risen from the table and sat by the fire, *Smack* came to her again and told her that she could not avoid those fits next week unless she left home. She—

She told him she would not, and told him to do his worst. Then he said, "When was *Catch* with you?" She said today, and that he threatened to punish her strangely next week with sick fits; he said it was because I had caused you to break his leg. "But I hope you will break his neck, and someone will break yours, for you are all naught <note>original: "naught"; meaning wicked, worthless, or evil.</note>." *Smack* said he would hunt <note>original: "course"; to pursue or chase, as in a hunt.</note> *Catch* for it, and guaranteed he would never come to her again once he had finished with him. Then she said, "Tell me when my fits will begin, and when they shall end?" He said that they would begin on *Monday* morning and end a week later <note>original: "that Day seven Night"; an archaic way of saying one week from that day.</note> in the morning. "I suppose you will have a week of them then?" she said. "Yes," said he, "and your body will be sore all the next week after."

On *Saturday*, which was the next day, she had several fits and was brought out of them by *Agnes Samuel*’s charge <note>This refers to the practice of forcing the suspected witch to command the spirits to leave the victim.</note>, and she had more fits on *Sunday*.

On *Monday*, when her troublesome fits were supposed to begin, she grew very sick and uneasy in the morning, though she kept her senses all week. Mrs. *Elizabeth* was also troubled this week with very sudden and extraordinary fits, her body being more severely twisted than it had been for several months before. This made us conclude that something more than ordinary was at work, as it was impossible she should endure such violent suffering <note>original: "Passions"; in this context, it refers to fits or intense physical suffering.</note> for long.

On *Saturday* the tenth of *March*, as she sat at supper, it seemed by the motion of her hands and head and her humming that she wanted to speak to something on the table, but her mouth was shut tight. Then she lamented sadly but could not express her grief. Soon after, she fell into such a troublesome fit that she could not sit on her stool; then *Agnes Samuel* was asked to hold her, which she did, and in a little time she began to grow more quiet. And her sister *Grace* was even more troubled than she was, so she set—

As she was holding Miss <note>original: "Mrs."; in the 16th and 17th centuries, "Mistress" was a title of respect used for young girls of high social standing, not only married women.</note> *Grace* in her arms, Miss *Elizabeth*, who sat close by her, suddenly said with a great deal of anger, "Now I can see the young witch in my fit, which I never could do before in a fit." The company wondered to hear the young child call the maid a witch, for she had never before given her, her mother, or anyone else an ill word, either in or out of her fit. Therefore, they concluded something extraordinary was at work. 

Then she said, "My sister *Jane's* devil <note>The "devil" refers to a familiar spirit, an entity believed to serve a witch.</note> told me as I sat at supper that I must scratch the young witch." As soon as she had said so, she slipped from the bench and fell upon her knees, for she was not able to stand. She caught the maid by the hand—the one standing next to her and holding her sister—and scratched one of her hands violently. She said it was the maid who had bewitched her and her sisters, or they would have been well long before. "O, you young witch! O, you young witch! Shame <note>original: "Fie"; an exclamation of disgust or reproach.</note> on you, shame on you! Who ever heard of a young witch before?"

When she had rested <note>original: "breathed"; paused to catch her breath.</note> a while, she fell upon her again and said it was her sister *Jane's* devil (who was not known to lie) who commanded her to scratch her. "For," she said, "I would not have scratched you, and it is against my will; but the devil makes me scratch you, and holds out my arms and bends my fingers. Otherwise, I would not do it, as I am now myself." All this while, the maid still held the other child in her arm, never offering to pull her hand away from her, but cried out sadly, begging the Lord to pity her.

Then someone who sat nearby asked her to say whether she thought the child scratched her of her own accord or not. She answered that she thought not. "Indeed," she said, "I know she did not, and that it is not her own mind to scratch me thus." When the child was weary of scratching the second...

<meta>This page continues the account of the Throckmorton children's fits and their accusations against a family servant. # WITCHCRAFT, etc. <note>original: "&c."</note>

At the same time, she held out her hands and said, "Look here, the Devil says I must scratch her no more now, for my fingers are stretched out so straight that I cannot bend them." This was apparent when she held out her hand. Then she rubbed her hand upon the maid’s hand that was bleeding slightly, and wiped the blood upon her own hands; she did this often while she talked with her. <note>Scratching a suspected witch until they bled was a common folk practice intended to provide temporary relief from a curse.</note>

Immediately, when the scratching was ended, the child began to weep. Crying, she said to the maid, "I would not have scratched you, but the Devil made me, whether I wanted to or not. Oh, I wish you had never deserved to be treated this way!" She complained as if she herself had committed some great offense.

When this fit of weeping was over, she began to urge her <note>original: "exhort"; to strongly encourage or urge someone to do something.</note> to change her ways, raising her voice with such a strong desire for the maid's improvement that the like was never heard from a child’s mouth. "Oh," said the child, "I wish you had the grace to repent for your wickedness, so that your soul might be saved. For you have forsaken your God and given yourself to the Devil. Oh, oh! If only you had known how precious your soul was, you never would have given it to the Devil. You need to pray night and day for God’s favor again; otherwise, your soul shall be damned in Hell for ever. You often pray here at home when we pray, and likewise at Church, but you pray in vain because you do not pray with your heart. But I will pray for you with all my heart, and I will forgive you, and ask all my sisters and all my friends to forgive you, if you will confess your fault. But you have a hard heart, and the Devil holds your heart and will not allow you to confess it. But you must confess it, whether you want to or not, when your time comes. But oh! I wish you would confess it now, so that your soul might be saved."

<summary>Elizabeth Throckmorton, after being compelled by 'the Devil' to scratch the maid, tearfully begs the woman to repent and save her soul from damnation. The child claims the maid's prayers are insincere because the Devil has hardened her heart against confession.</summary>
